Weak solutions of Navier-Stokes Equation with purely discontinuous Lévy Noise
Zdzisław Brzeźniak, Tomasz Kosmala, Elżbieta Motyl, Paul Razafimandimby

Abstract
In this paper we prove the existence of weak martingale solutions to the stochastic Navier-Stokes Equations driven by pure jump Lévy processes. Our proof consists of two parts. In the first one, mostly classical, we recall a priori estimates, from the paper by the third named author, for solutions to suitable constructed Galerkin approximations and we use the Jakubowski-Skorokhod Theorem to find a sequence of processes on a new probability space convergent point-wise to a limit process. In the second one, we show that the limit process is a weak martingale solution to the SNSEs by using an approach of Kallianpur and Xiong. The core of this method consists of a proof that a certain natural process on the new probability space is a purely discontinuous martingale and then to use a suitable representation theorem. In this way we propose a method of proving solutions to stochastic PDEs which is different from the method used recently by the first and fourth named author in their joint paper with E. Hausenblas, see [ 10 ] .



1
Introduction

In this paper we consider the following Navier-Stokes Equations (NSEs), in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
d=2
or
d=3
, driven by a Poisson Random Measure

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u-\Delta u+\langle u,\nabla\rangle u+\nabla p=f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(t,u,y)\,\tilde{\eta}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}y)\\
\divergence u=0,\\
u(0)=u_{0},\end{cases}

(1.1)

where

\displaystyle u\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},

\displaystyle p\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

are respectively the velocity and pressure of the fluid. The symbol
\tilde{\eta}
denotes a compensated time homogeneous Poisson random measure
on
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times Y,\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathscr{Y})
over a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
intensity measure of the form
\Leb\otimes\nu
, where
\Leb
is the Lebesgue measure on the
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
\nu
is a
\sigma
-finite measure on the
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{Y}
, see Definition
2.2
.
We omit here the dependence of the sample element
\omega\in\Omega
.

Before we describe the field of Stochastic Navier Stokes Equations (SNSEs) driven by jump processes, let us spend some time on describing the literature concerned with the existence theory of the Stochastic Navier Stokes Equations driven by Gaussian processes. To the best of our knowledge, the first paper on this topic was written by Bensoussan and Temam
[
3
]
who considered the SNSEs driven by the additive Wiener process. This line of research was continued by Vishik and Fursikov, see e.g. their monograph
[
62
]
. The first paper which consider the SNSEs driven by the so called transport noise was the paper
[
8
]
, by the first named author, Flandoli and Capiński. This direction was later taken up by Flandoli and Gatarek
[
31
]
, who by the way, also treated other types of gaussian noises. All these papers and others, which we have not cited, have dealt with NSEs in bounded domains. The first paper which dealt with the exietnce of solutions to the SNSEs (as well as Euler Equations) in unbounded domains was the paper
[
21
]
which treated multiplicative but not of transport type gaussian noise. After that Rozovski and Mikulevicius published the paper
[
46
]
which studied the SNSEs in the whole space.
The method used by these authors was different and used weak topolopogies instead of weighted spaces used in the former paper. The latter paper was subsequently generalized by the first and third named authors in
[
17
]
, who proved the existence of weak martingale solutions to SNSEs (in both dimensions
d=2
and
d=3
for general unbounded domains. One important improvement was the use of the Jakukowski’s generalization
[
37
]
to a class of non-metric spaces of the Skorokhod representation theorem
[
57
]
. We have not mentioned papers related to qualitative behaviours of SNSEs as for instance the existence and properties of attractors, the existence, uniqueness and properties invariant measures and of stationary solutions. We want to finish this part of our Introduction about very recent developments. In particular about the question of regularization by noise, see
e.g.
[
32
]
and references therein, the non-uniqueness of very weak solutions in the sense of convex integration,
L^{p}
-theory of SNSEs, including transport noise, see e.g.
[
20
]
,
[
2
]
and references therein, as well the geometric approach to transport noise, see e.g.
[
24
]
and references therein.

There exists also a vast amount of results related to the stochastic Navier-Stokes equations driven by the Lévy processes called also jump noise. The existence of solutions, the long-time behaviour of these solutions, in particular the existence and uniqueness of invariant measure associated to the solutions, has been the subjects of extensive mathematical analysis, which have generated several important results, see
[
12
,
25
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
47
,
48
,
56
,
63
]
among others.

To the best of our knowledge, the first papers about the Stochastic Navier-Stokes Equations driven by Lévy type noise were articles by Dong Zhao and his collaborators. In particular, Dong Zhao and his collaborators proved the existence of weak solutions of 2D and 3D Navier-Stokes with Lévy noise in
[
26
]
,
[
25
]
, established the Large Deviation Principles of Lévy noise driven 2D Navier-Stokes equations in
[
63
]
, and show the existence of invariant measure of the 2D Navier-Stokes equatiosn driven by
\alpha
-stable Lévy noise in
[
27
]
. Then, there were two papers
[
13
]
and
[
56
]
by, respectively, Brzeźniak et al and Striharan, which used a similar approach, called Barbu method adapted to the jump case. These two papers and the ones by Dong Zhao and his collaborators do not use arguments based on tightness or compactness theorems.

These papers were followed by two fundamental papers
[
47
]
and
[
48
]
, who extended her own works
[
16
]
and
[
17
]
from the Wiener processes to the full Lévy case. Recently,
Brzeźniak, Peng and Zhai proved in
[
19
]
the existence of weak and strong solutions to the 2D stochastic Navier Stokes Equations driven by pure jump Lévy processes for the initial data from the solenoidal versions of either the Lebesgue space
L^{2}
or the Sobolev space
H^{1}
. They used an
ingenuous proof based on the classical Banach Fixed Point Theorem.
Most importantly, they also established and fully proved the Large Deviations Principle (what was the main motivation of that paper). This paper was generalized in
[
53
]
by Peng, Yang and Zhai by removing some "atypical" assumptions. The recent literature on the topic of SPDEs driven by Lévy processes of jump type includes also (as examples) the following aricles:

[
23
]
by Chen, Wang and Wang,
[
50
]
by Nguyen, Tawri and Temam,
[
40
]
by Kumar and Mohan, and
[
49
]
Ndongmo Ngana, Deugoue and Tachim Medjo.

Despite this vast amount of papers, there are still some questions, which are well-understood for the Wiener process driven Navier-Stokes equations, remain unanswered for the Navier-Stokes driven by pure jump noise, e.g. uniqueness of invariant measure for 2D Navier-Stokes driven by a noise of the form
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}L_{k}
, where
(L_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is sequence of i.i.d. Lévy processes and only finitely many of the
\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
are non-zero, and the existence of weak martingale solutions via a martingale representation theorem. The main reason for this is that many of the mathematical tools which are useful for the analysis of Wiener processes driven SPDEs might not be applicable to SPDEs with Lévy noise. Also, there are some important results which are true for SPDEs driven by specific classes of Lévy noise but cannot be applied to SPDEs driven by a different type of Lévy noise, for instance the earlier cited paper
[
27
]
, who studied the existence of invariant measures of stochastic 2D Navier-Stokes equations driven by
\alpha
-stable processes.

Our main (and modest) goal in this paper is to establish the existence of a global weak solution of (
1.1
).
In the construction of a weak solution in the probabilistic sense, an important rôle is play an appropriate representation theorems. While considering SPDEs equations driven by Gaussian processes, the well-known theorem on the representation of the square integrable continuous martingales as the Itô stochastic integrals with respect to Wiener processes is used, see e.g.
[
31
]
,
[
17
]
.
Following this classical idea, in our construction of a weak solution to problem (
1.1
), we use the representation theorem for square integrable purely discontinuous martingales as the stochastic integrals with respect to the Poisson random measures, see

[
39
]
,
[
34
, Section II. 7]
and reference therein. This approach enables us, in particular, to indicate explicitly appropriate Poisson random measure being a part of the weak solution to problem (
1.1
), see Definition
3.9
. This is the main novelty of the present paper.

We will use the Skorokhod Theorem in the Jakubowski version
[
37
]
,
but the proof that the limiting process is a solution to equation (
1.1
) is completely different from the one used in the previously mentioned paper
[
10
]
.
Our method is based on representation of pure jump martingales as stochastic integrals with respect to Poisson Random measure. A byproduct of our work is that we have prepared ground for treating other SPDEs driven by Lévy processes.
The reason we wanted to find a proof which does not rely on the method used in the paper
[
10
]
by the first and fourth named authors is that we are no longer convinced that part (iii) of Theorem C.1 therein is correct, as it was pointed out to us by M. Ondreját
[
51
]
.
We are grateful to M. Ondreját for his personal information and to A. Debussche for related discussion.

Let us describe the content of the present paper in more detail. We prove, under fairly general assumption on the noise coefficients and the measure
\nu
that the system (
1.1
) has a global weak solution. The term
weak solution
is understood in the sense of both the Stochastic Analysis and the Partial Differential Equations. That is we look for both the velocity
u
, a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
, where
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, satisfying the usual condition and a Poisson Random Measure
\eta
with the prescribed Lévy measure
\nu
. The velocity
u
is required to satisfies the variational formulation of (
1.1
).
A precise formulation of the definition of a solution, we use the term "martingale solution", is given in Definition
3.9
and the precise formulation of the main result is given in Theorem
3.11
.
The presentation from sections
3
and
4
follows mostly the papers
[
47
]
and
[
48
]
by the third named author. However, because of some small but important changes and for the sake of the completeness, we include as much details as possible.
Moreover, we prove our main result by using a theorem on representation of pure jump martingales by stochastic integrals driven by compensated Poisson Random Measure. While this kind of representation theorem is well-known and understood for martingales with continuous paths, it is known to hold only for purely discontinuous processes with values in a co-nuclear space, see
[
39
, Th. 3.4.7]
. Our second main contribution, which is interesting in itself, is the proof of a Representation Theorem for purely discontinuous martingales for Hilbert-space valued processes. Although we closely follow the presentation in
[
39
]
, the proof is non-trivial, see Appendix
D
.

The layout of this paper can be summarized as follows. In Section
2
we recall and present some preliminary notions and results that are needed in the subsequent sections. Section 3 is devoted to the statement of the standing assumptions and the main result of the paper.
We start the proof of our main result in Section
4
where we introduce the Galerkin approximation of the problem (
1.1
). Still in Section
4
, we derive several important uniform estimates for the solutions of the Galerkin approximation, which will be used to show that the family of the approximate solutions’ laws are tight on a space
\mathscr{Z}_{T}
introduced in (
3.3
). This tightness result along with the Skorokhod-Jakubowski Theorem enables us to construct new probability space on which almost sure convergence of a sequence of random variables (with the same laws as the solutions to the Galerkin approximation problems) to the candidate solution
\overline{u}
holds.
Section 5 is devoted to one of the crucial and technical steps of the proof. There we prove that the
processes

\displaystyle{\overline{M}}(t)

\displaystyle=\overline{u}(t)\,-\overline{u}(0)-\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{A}\overline{u}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}{B}\bigl(\overline{u}(s)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\in[0,T].

and

\displaystyle\overline{N}_{\phi}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\langle\overline{u}(t),\phi\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-34.56949pt\hskip 34.56949pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-34.56949ptU^{\prime}\hskip 34.56949ptU}}}{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\langle\overline{u}(t),\phi\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-34.56949pt\hskip 34.56949pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-34.56949ptU^{\prime}\hskip 34.56949ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\langle\overline{u}(t),\phi\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-29.12093pt\hskip 29.12093pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-29.12093ptU^{\prime}\hskip 29.12093ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle\overline{u}(t),\phi\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.23514pt\hskip 27.23514pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-27.23514ptU^{\prime}\hskip 27.23514ptU}}}-\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\langle u_{0},\phi\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-28.39127pt\hskip 28.39127pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-28.39127ptU^{\prime}\hskip 28.39127ptU}}}{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\langle u_{0},\phi\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-28.39127pt\hskip 28.39127pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-28.39127ptU^{\prime}\hskip 28.39127ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\langle u_{0},\phi\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.48093pt\hskip 23.48093pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-23.48093ptU^{\prime}\hskip 23.48093ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle u_{0},\phi\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-22.13332pt\hskip 22.13332pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-22.13332ptU^{\prime}\hskip 22.13332ptU}}}

\displaystyle+2\int_{0}^{t}\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\bigl\langle\mathscr{A}\overline{u}(s)+B(\overline{u}(s))-f(s),\phi\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-114.91318pt\hskip 114.91318pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-114.91318ptU^{\prime}\hskip 114.91318ptU}}}{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigl\langle\mathscr{A}\overline{u}(s)+B(\overline{u}(s))-f(s),\phi\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-114.91318pt\hskip 114.91318pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-114.91318ptU^{\prime}\hskip 114.91318ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bigl\langle\mathscr{A}\overline{u}(s)+B(\overline{u}(s))-f(s),\phi\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-86.97643pt\hskip 86.97643pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-86.97643ptU^{\prime}\hskip 86.97643ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigl\langle\mathscr{A}\overline{u}(s)+B(\overline{u}(s))-f(s),\phi\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-77.55106pt\hskip 77.55106pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-77.55106ptU^{\prime}\hskip 77.55106ptU}}}\;\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\bigl\langle\overline{u}(s),\phi\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-35.64587pt\hskip 35.64587pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-35.64587ptU^{\prime}\hskip 35.64587ptU}}}{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigl\langle\overline{u}(s),\phi\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-35.64587pt\hskip 35.64587pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-35.64587ptU^{\prime}\hskip 35.64587ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bigl\langle\overline{u}(s),\phi\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-29.8744pt\hskip 29.8744pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-29.8744ptU^{\prime}\hskip 29.8744ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigl\langle\overline{u}(s),\phi\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.76755pt\hskip 27.76755pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-27.76755ptU^{\prime}\hskip 27.76755ptU}}}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}{\bigl(F(s,\overline{u}(s),y),\phi\bigr)}_{H}^{2}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s,\;\;t\in[0,T],\,\phi\in U

are martingales with respect to standard augmentation of the filtration generated by
\overline{u}
.

In Section
6
we use these results along with the Martingale Representation Theorem for purely discontinuous martingales
D.6
, due to Kallianpur and Xiong, see
[
39
, Theorem 3.4.7]
, in order to construct the desired martingale solutions. The analyses in Sections
5
and
6
are nontrivial and require long, tedious, and technical arguments. They are also the main difference between the current paper and previous work on global weak solutions of stochastic Navier-Stokes with L’evy noise. In the appendices, we state and prove several results, some of which are well-known and some less known, that are necessary for our analysis. In particular, we establish in Appendix
D
a Martingale Representation Theorem for purely discontinuous martingales. This theorem is interesting in itself and will be useful in the proof of existence of martingale solutions of other problems.

Notation.

Throughout this paper we set
\mathbb{R}_{+}=[0,\infty)
and
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\ast}=(0,\infty)
. We denote by
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and by
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
the sets
[0,\infty]
and
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, respectively. We also denote by
\mathbb{N}
and
\overline{\mathbb{N}}
the sets
\{0,1,2,3,\ldots\}
and
\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\}
, respectively.
For a nonempty subset
C\subset[0,\infty]
we set

C^{2}_{+}\coloneqq\bigl\{(s,t)\in C^{2}:s\leq t\bigr\}.

Let
X
be a topological Hausdorff space,
T\in(0,\infty)

and
f\colon[0,T]\to X
a càdlàg function, that is
f
is right continuous and has left limit at each point
t\in[0,T]
. We put

\displaystyle f_{-}(t)=f(t-)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\lim_{s\nearrow t}f(s),&\mbox{ if }t\in(0,T],\\
f(0)&\mbox{ if }t=0,\end{cases}

Moreover, if
X
is a vector space, then we put

\displaystyle\Delta f(t)=f(t)-f(t-),\qquad t\in[0,T],

(1.2)

By
\mathbb{D}([0,T];X)
we will denote the space of all càdlàg functions
f\colon[0,T]\to X
. In the special case of
X=\mathbb{R}
we will use a simple notation

\mathbb{D}([0,T])\coloneqq\mathbb{D}([0,T];\mathbb{R}).

We also introduce the following useful notation. For
t\in[0,T]
we put

\mathbb{D}_{t}^{c}([0,T];X)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathbb{D}([0,T];X),&\mbox{ if }t\in\{0,T\},\\
\bigl\{x\in\mathbb{D}([0,T];X):\;\Delta x(t)=0\}&\mbox{ if }t\in(0,T).\end{cases}

(1.3)

By
[
35
, Cor. I.1.25]
, if
\xi
is a càdlàg adapted process with values in a separable Banach space
X
, then both processes

\xi_{-}
and
\Delta\xi
are optional.

By an
evanescent
set we mean a random set
A\subset[0,T]\times\Omega
, whose projection on
\Omega
, i.e. the set
\bigl\{\omega\in\Omega:\exists t\in[0,T]:(t,\omega)\in A\bigr\}
, is a
\mathbb{P}
-null set, see
[
35
, I.1.10]
.

To avoid any ambiguity, let us emphasise that given a property
R
,
whenever we write

\mathbb{P}\mbox{-a.s. }R\mbox{ holds for every }t\in[0,T],

(1.4)

or

\mathbb{P}\mbox{-a.s., for every }t\in[0,T],\;\mbox{ property $R$ holds }

(1.5)

we mean that the set

\Omega_{R}\coloneqq\bigl\{\omega\in\Omega:\mbox{ for every }t\in[0,T],\mbox{ property }R\mbox{ holds for }(t,\omega)\bigr\}

(1.6)

is
\mathbb{P}
-full, i.e. the random set

A\coloneqq\bigl\{(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega:\mbox{ property }R\mbox{ does not hold for }(t,\omega)\bigr\}

(1.7)

is an evanescent set.

On the other hand, when we write

for every
t\in[0,T],

R
holds
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

(1.8)

we mean that for every
t\in[0,T]
, the set

\bigl\{\omega\in\Omega:\mbox{ property }R\mbox{ holds for }(t,\omega)\bigr\}\mbox{ is a $\mathbb{P}$-full set.}

For two normed vector spaces
X
and
Y
,
\mathscr{L}(X,Y)
denotes the space of bounded linear operators from
X
to
Y
and the operator norm is denoted by
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathscr{L}(X,Y)}
. When
Y=X
we will simply write

\mathscr{L}(X)
and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathscr{L}(X)}
.
By
X^{\prime}
we will denote dual space of
X
, i.e.
X^{\prime}=\mathscr{L}(X,\mathbb{R})
,
and by
\mathchoice{{\hskip 9.80699pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\bigl\langle\cdot,\cdot\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.80699pt\hskip-16.11116ptX^{\prime}\hskip 16.11116ptX}}}{{\hskip 9.80699pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigl\langle\cdot,\cdot\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.80699pt\hskip-16.11116ptX^{\prime}\hskip 16.11116ptX}}}{{\hskip 8.65288pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bigl\langle\cdot,\cdot\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.7917pt\hskip 14.7917pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.65288pt\hskip-14.7917ptX^{\prime}\hskip 14.7917ptX}}}{{\hskip 8.65288pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigl\langle\cdot,\cdot\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.37509pt\hskip 14.37509pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.65288pt\hskip-14.37509ptX^{\prime}\hskip 14.37509ptX}}}
we will denote the duality between
{X}^{\prime}
and
X
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section we introduce few definitions related to Poisson Random Measures and introduce technical terms and definitions related to the theory of Navier-Stokes equations.

2.1
Poisson Random Measures

As mentioned in the introduction of this paper we are interested in the analysis of the Navier-Stokes equation with Lévy noise. Similarly to the papers by Brzeźniak and Motyl
[
17
]
and by Motyl
[
47
,
48
]
we consider a mutliplicative noise described by means of Poisson Random Measures. In this section we recall two concepts, namely that of a Poisson Random Measure (PRM) by closely following
[
34
]
and its extension to a PRM with respect to a given filtration.
To this aim, let
M_{\overline{\mathbb{N}}}(E)
denote the set of all
\overline{\mathbb{N}}
-valued measures on a measurable space
(E,\mathscr{E})
. The space
M_{\overline{\mathbb{N}}}(E)
has the natural
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{M}_{\overline{\mathbb{N}}}(E)
generated by the following family os maps

\mbox{
$M_{\overline{\mathbb{N}}}(E)\ni\mu\mapsto\mu(A)\in\overline{\mathbb{N}}$
},\;\;A\in\mathscr{E}.

We start by recalling the following definition of a Poisson random measure. Our expositure is based on
[
34
, Def. 8.1]
.

Definition 2.1
.

A measurable function

\eta\colon\Omega\to M_{\overline{\mathbb{N}}}(E)

(2.1)

is a Poisson random measure if and only if

(i)

for any
A\in\mathscr{E}
, the function

\eta(A)\colon\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\eta(\omega)(A)\in\overline{\mathbb{N}}

is a Poisson random variable with parameter
\mathbb{E}\left[\eta(A)\right]
if
\mathbb{E}\left[\eta(A)\right]<\infty
, and
\eta(A)=\infty

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. otherwise.

(ii)

For every finite family
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots A_{n}\in\mathscr{E}
of pairwise disjoint sets, the random variables
\eta(A_{1}),\ldots,\eta(A_{n})
are independent.

By the
intensity measure
of a PRM
\eta
we understand the measure

\mu\colon\mathscr{E}\ni A\mapsto\mathbb{E}\left[\eta(A)\right]\in\overline{\mathbb{N}}_{+}.

(2.2)

We will always assume that the filtered probability space

(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P},\mathbb{F})
satisfies the so-called
usual hypothesis
, i.e.

the filtration
\mathbb{F}
is right-continuous, that is

\mathscr{F}_{t}=\bigcap\limits_{u>t}\mathscr{F}_{u},\qquad\text{for all }t\in[0,\infty)\,,

and every
\mathbb{P}
-null, i.e.
\mathbb{P}
-negligible, element
A\in\mathscr{F}
, belongs to
\mathscr{F}_{0}
.

Next, similarly to the concepts of a Wiener or a Lévy process with respect to a filtration, we introduce a notion of a time-dependent Poisson random measure, see e.g.

[
11
, Def. 2.3]
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(Y,\mathscr{Y})
be a measurable space and let

E=\mathbb{R}_{+}\times Y\mbox{ and }\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathscr{Y}.

(2.3)

Let
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\mathbb{F}=(\mathscr{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a filtration on
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})
satisfying the usual conditions.
A measurable function

\eta\colon\Omega\to M_{\overline{\mathbb{N}}}(E)

(2.4)

is called a Poisson random measure over

(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
if and only it satisfies the conditions (i) and (ii) in Definition
2.1
as well as the following two additional conditions:

(iii)

For all
A\in\mathscr{Y}
and
t\geq 0
the
function

\eta((0,t]\times A)\colon\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\eta(\omega)((0,t]\times A)\in\overline{\mathbb{N}}

is
\mathscr{F}_{t}
-measurable.

(iv)

For all
A\in\mathscr{Y}
and
(s,t)\in[0,\infty)^{2}_{+}
the random variable
\eta((s,t]\times A)
is independent of the
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{F}_{s}
.

A Poisson random measure
\eta
as in (
2.4
) will be called a time homogeneous Poisson random measure on
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times Y,\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathscr{Y})
over
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
if and only if its intensity measure
\mu
is of the form
\Leb\otimes\nu
, where
\Leb
is the Lebesgue measure on
the
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
\nu
is a
\sigma
-finite measure on the
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{Y}
.

For more background information about the integration with respect to point processes and random measures, see the Ikeda-Watanabe monograph
[
34
, p. 61-63]
,
where random measures
\eta_{p}
are associated to point processes
p
. We summarise that background in Appendix
C
. Here we present integration with respect to Poisson random measures and define random measures of class (QL).

Given a Poisson point processes
p
with its domain denoted by
\mathrm{Dom}(p)
and the associated Poisson random measure
\eta_{p}
,
we define the following three classes of random functions, see
[
34
]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigl\{f\colon[0,\infty)\times Y\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}:f\mbox{ is }\mathbb{F}-\mbox{predictable and for every }t>0,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}|f(s,y,\omega)|\,\eta_{p}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)<\infty\mbox{ a.s.}\Bigr\},

where we put

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}|f(s,y,\omega)|\,\eta_{p}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\coloneqq\sum_{s\in(0,t]\cap\mathrm{Dom}(p)}|f(s,p(s),\omega)|,\;\;t\geq 0.

(2.5)

For
k=1,2
, with
\mu
being the intensity measure of
\eta_{p}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{p}^{k}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigl\{f\colon[0,\infty)\times Y\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}:f\mbox{ is }\mathbb{F}-\mbox{predictable},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}|f(s,y,\omega)|^{k}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)<\infty,\;\;t>0\Bigr\}.

For
f\in\mathbf{F}_{p}
the integral on the LHS of (
2.6
) is well-defined:

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}f(s,y,\omega)\,\eta_{p}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)=\sum_{s\in(0,t]\cap\mathrm{Dom}(p)}f(s,p(s),\omega),\;\;t\geq 0.

(2.6)

One can show that

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}|f(s,y,\omega)|\,\eta_{p}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}|f(s,y,\omega)|\,\mu(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y).

(2.7)

In order to define the integral with respect to the compensated Poisson random measure defined by

\tilde{\eta}_{p}:=\eta_{p}-\mu,

(2.8)

we first define it for a random function
f\in\mathbf{F}_{p}^{1}\cap\mathbf{F}_{p}^{2}
by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}f(s,y,\omega)\,\tilde{\eta}_{p}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}f(s,y,\omega)\,\eta_{p}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)

(2.9)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}f(s,y,\omega)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y),\;\;t\geq 0.

Let us point out that in view of (
2.7
),
\mathbf{F}_{p}^{1}\subset\mathbf{F}_{p}
and so both integrals on the RHS of (
2.9
) above exist
\mathbb{P}
- a.s.
The integral defined by (
2.9
) can be naturally extended to the whole class
\mathbf{F}_{p}^{2}
, see
[
34
]
, section II.3 and the resulting integral
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}f(s,y,\omega)\,\tilde{\eta}_{p}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)
is a square-integrable martingale. In particular,

\mathbb{E}\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}f(s,y,\omega)\,\tilde{\eta}_{p}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}|f(s,y,\omega)|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y),\;\;t\geq 0.

(2.10)

We will also use a more general object, namely random measures of class (QL), see Definition
C.15
for more details.

Definition 2.3
.

Assume that
(Y,\mathscr{Y})
be a measurable space. A random measure

\eta\colon\Omega\to M_{\overline{\mathbb{N}}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times Y)

is of (QL) class if and only if

(i)

it is
\sigma
-finite i.e. there exists a sequence
(U_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
of elements of the family
\Gamma_{\eta}
defined by

\Gamma_{\eta}:=\{A\in\mathscr{E}:\mathbb{E}\,\left\lvert\eta([0,t]\times A)\right\rvert<\infty\mbox{ for all }t\geq 0\},

(2.11)

such that
U_{n}\nearrow E
,

(ii)

there exists a
\sigma
-finite random measure

\widehat{\eta}:\Omega\times\bigl(\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathscr{Y}\bigr)\to\mathbb{R},

such that
\tilde{\eta}=\eta-\widehat{\eta}
is a martingale random measure,

(iii)

for any
A\in\Gamma_{\eta}
the process
the process

\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\ni(t,\omega)\mapsto\widehat{\eta}(\omega;[0,t]\times A)

is continuous.

2.2
Useful Sobolev spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

In this subsection we introduce several definitions and concepts that are related to the mathematical analysis of the Navier-Stokes equations.
Our results, with some little extra work, can be shown to be valid in every domain with sufficiently smooth boundary. However, we have decided to avoid unnecessary complications and present all results
in the special case of the Euclidean spaces
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

By the symbol
{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

we will denote the standard Sobolev space of all functions
u\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\coloneqq{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
whose weak derivatives
D_{i}u=\frac{\partial u}{\partial{x}_{i}}
exist and belong to
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
i=1,\ldots,d
.
For
u,v\in\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we put

{\bigl(\nabla u,\nabla v\bigr)}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\partial u}{\partial{x}_{i}}\cdot\frac{\partial v}{\partial{x}_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\partial u_{j}}{\partial{x}_{i}}\frac{\partial v_{j}}{\partial{x}_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}x,

where the dot
\cdot
in the middle integral denotes the scalar product in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The space
\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
endowed with the following scalar product

{\bigl(u,v\bigr)}_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\coloneqq{\bigl(u,v\bigr)}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+{\bigl(\nabla u,\nabla v\bigr)}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},\qquad u,v\in\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

is a Hilbert space.

The fractional order Sobolev spaces
\mathbb{H}^{\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\coloneqq{H}^{\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, are defined by means of the Fourier transform, see
[
55
,
42
,
44
]
.
Note that for
\theta=1
the two definitions yield the same Hilbert space.

Next, we denote by
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of all
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}
-class
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued functions with compact support. We will also use the following spaces

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{u\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d}):\,\,\diver u=0\},

(2.12)

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mbox{the closure of $\mathscr{V}$ in $\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$},

(2.13)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mbox{the closure of $\mathscr{V}$ in ${\mathbb{H}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

(2.14)

Before we continue let us formulate the following fundamental property of the space
\mathscr{V}
, a property that is the basis for the Helmholtz decomposition, i.e.

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(x)\cdot\nabla p(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=0\mbox{ for all }u\in\mathscr{V},\;p\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}).

(2.15)

We endow the space
H
with the scalar product, and hence the norm, inherited from the space
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We
denote them respectively by
{\bigl(\cdot,\cdot\bigr)}_{H}
and
\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|\cdot|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.33336pt\hskip 8.33336pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.33336pt\hskip 8.33336ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|\cdot|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.33336pt\hskip 8.33336pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.33336pt\hskip 8.33336ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|\cdot|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-7.125pt\hskip 7.125pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-7.125pt\hskip 7.125ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|\cdot|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-6.87506pt\hskip 6.87506pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-6.87506pt\hskip 6.87506ptH}}}
, i.e.

\displaystyle{\bigl(u,v\bigr)}_{H}\coloneqq{\bigl(u,v\bigr)}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}},\qquad\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.47806pt\hskip 9.47806pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.47806pt\hskip 9.47806ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.81377pt\hskip 8.81377pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.81377pt\hskip 8.81377ptH}}}\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.47806pt\hskip 9.47806pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.47806pt\hskip 9.47806pt{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.81377pt\hskip 8.81377pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.81377pt\hskip 8.81377pt{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}}},\qquad u,v\in H.

One can show that the above spaces can be characterised in a different way, see
[
59
, Theorem 1.1/1.4 and 1.1/1.6]
:

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\{u\in\mathbb{L}^{2}:\divergence u=0\},

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\{u\in\mathbb{H}^{1}:\divergence u=0\}.

We endow the space
V
with the scalar product
inherited from
{\mathbb{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e.

\displaystyle{\bigl(u,v\bigr)}_{V}\coloneqq{\bigl(u,v\bigr)}_{H}+{\bigl(\nabla u,\nabla v\bigr)}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

We will denote the norm induced by the scalar product
{\bigl(\cdot,\cdot\bigr)}_{V}
by
\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|\cdot|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.33336pt\hskip 8.33336pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.33336pt\hskip 8.33336ptV}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|\cdot|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.33336pt\hskip 8.33336pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.33336pt\hskip 8.33336ptV}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|\cdot|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-7.125pt\hskip 7.125pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-7.125pt\hskip 7.125ptV}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|\cdot|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-6.87506pt\hskip 6.87506pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-6.87506pt\hskip 6.87506ptV}}}
, i.e.

\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449ptV}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449ptV}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.47806pt\hskip 9.47806pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.47806pt\hskip 9.47806ptV}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.81377pt\hskip 8.81377pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.81377pt\hskip 8.81377ptV}}}\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.47806pt\hskip 9.47806pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.47806pt\hskip 9.47806ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.81377pt\hskip 8.81377pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.81377pt\hskip 8.81377ptH}}}+\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.52437pt\hskip 17.52437pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.52437pt\hskip 17.52437pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.8046pt\hskip 15.8046pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.8046pt\hskip 15.8046pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}.

(2.16)

We also define the following scale of Hilbert spaces

{V}_{\theta}\coloneqq\mbox{the closure of $\mathscr{V}$ in $\mathbb{H}^{\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$},\;\;\theta\in[0,\infty).

(2.17)

In particular,
V_{0}=H
.
Using the method of proof of
[
59
, Theorem 1.1]
one can show that

\displaystyle V_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{H}^{\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap H,\;\;\;\;\theta\in[0,\infty).

From now on we choose and fix a real number
s
such that

\displaystyle s>\frac{d}{2}+1.

(2.18)

It follows from the definitions (
2.14
) and (
2.17
) of the space
V
and the space
V_{s}
, respectively, that

V_{s}\subset V\subset H
and

\displaystyle\mbox{ the embedding }V_{s}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow V\mbox{ is continuous},

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mbox{ the embedding }V_{s}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow H\mbox{ is continuous}.

(2.20)

Now, let
{\mathscr{C}}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the space of continuous and bounded
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued functions. Then, since
s>\frac{d}{2}+1
by the Sobolev Embedding Theorem we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{H}}^{s-1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(2.21)

Next, it is well known, see for instance
[
17
, Lemma C.1 ]
, that
there exists a Hilbert space
U
, with the inner product denoted by
{\bigl(\cdot,\cdot\bigr)}_{U}
such that

U\subset{V}_{s}\mbox{ densely}

(2.22)

and

\mbox{the natural embedding }\iota_{s}\colon U\hookrightarrow{V}_{s}\mbox{ is compact}.

(2.23)

We will denote by

\iota\colon U\hookrightarrow V\mbox{ and }i\colon U\hookrightarrow H

(2.24)

two other natural embeddings. Hence, in view of (
2.19
) we deduce that

U\hookrightarrow V\hookrightarrow H\mbox{ continuously }

(2.25)

with the the first embedding being compact.
Moreover, without loss of generality, we can suppose that the embedding

U\hookrightarrow H\mbox{ is a contraction}.

(2.26)

By
[
42
, Section 2.1]
, there exists an unbounded positive operator
L
in
H
, with domain
D(L)\subset H
, such that

D(L^{1/2})=U\mbox{ isometrically}.

(2.27)

In particular, the following identities hold

\displaystyle{\bigl(Lu,w\bigr)}_{H}

\displaystyle={\bigl(u,w\bigr)}_{U},\qquad u\in D(L),\quad w\in U,

(2.28)

\displaystyle|u|_{U}

\displaystyle=|L^{1/2}u|_{H},\qquad u\in U.

(2.29)

Let us observe that for each element
u\in H
there exists a unique functional
{u}^{\ast}\in U^{\prime}
such that

\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\bigl\langle{u}^{\ast},v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.75003pt\hskip 27.75003pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-27.75003ptU^{\prime}\hskip 27.75003ptU}}}{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigl\langle{u}^{\ast},v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.75003pt\hskip 27.75003pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-27.75003ptU^{\prime}\hskip 27.75003ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bigl\langle{u}^{\ast},v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.20154pt\hskip 23.20154pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-23.20154ptU^{\prime}\hskip 23.20154ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigl\langle{u}^{\ast},v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-21.99323pt\hskip 21.99323pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-21.99323ptU^{\prime}\hskip 21.99323ptU}}}\coloneqq{\bigl(u,v\bigr)}_{H},\qquad v\in U.

(2.30)

As usual, we identify the dual
{H}^{\prime}
with
{H}
and
in what follows we will identify
u\in H
with
u^{\ast}\in U^{\prime}
and therefore we have
{H}\hookrightarrow U^{\prime}
as well as the following equality

\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\bigl\langle u,v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.1528pt\hskip 23.1528pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-23.1528ptU^{\prime}\hskip 23.1528ptU}}}{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigl\langle u,v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.1528pt\hskip 23.1528pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-23.1528ptU^{\prime}\hskip 23.1528ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bigl\langle u,v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-19.02095pt\hskip 19.02095pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-19.02095ptU^{\prime}\hskip 19.02095ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigl\langle u,v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.81264pt\hskip 17.81264pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-17.81264ptU^{\prime}\hskip 17.81264ptU}}}={\bigl(u,v\bigr)}_{H},\qquad u\in H,\;\;v\in U.

(2.31)

Moreover, the dual space
{V}^{\prime}
can be identified with a subspace of
{U}^{\prime}
and hence we have the following generalized Gelfand triple

U\hookrightarrow{V}_{s}\hookrightarrow V\hookrightarrow H\cong H^{\prime}\hookrightarrow V^{\prime}\hookrightarrow{V}_{s}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow U^{\prime}

(2.32)

as well as the following identity linking the duality paring
\mathchoice{{\hskip 9.14935pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.14935pt\hskip-16.11116ptV^{\prime}\hskip 16.11116ptV}}}{{\hskip 9.14935pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.14935pt\hskip-16.11116ptV^{\prime}\hskip 16.11116ptV}}}{{\hskip 8.23274pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.7917pt\hskip 14.7917pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.23274pt\hskip-14.7917ptV^{\prime}\hskip 14.7917ptV}}}{{\hskip 8.23274pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle\cdot,\cdot\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.37509pt\hskip 14.37509pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.23274pt\hskip-14.37509ptV^{\prime}\hskip 14.37509ptV}}}{}
between spaces
V^{\prime}
and
V
with the
duality paring
\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\bigl\langle\cdot,\cdot\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-16.11116ptU^{\prime}\hskip 16.11116ptU}}}{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigl\langle\cdot,\cdot\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.11116pt\hskip 16.11116pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-16.11116ptU^{\prime}\hskip 16.11116ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bigl\langle\cdot,\cdot\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.7917pt\hskip 14.7917pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-14.7917ptU^{\prime}\hskip 14.7917ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigl\langle\cdot,\cdot\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-14.37509pt\hskip 14.37509pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-14.37509ptU^{\prime}\hskip 14.37509ptU}}}
between spaces
U^{\prime}
and
U
,

\mathchoice{{\hskip 9.14935pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle u,v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.1528pt\hskip 23.1528pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.14935pt\hskip-23.1528ptV^{\prime}\hskip 23.1528ptV}}}{{\hskip 9.14935pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle u,v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.1528pt\hskip 23.1528pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.14935pt\hskip-23.1528ptV^{\prime}\hskip 23.1528ptV}}}{{\hskip 8.23274pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle u,v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-19.02095pt\hskip 19.02095pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.23274pt\hskip-19.02095ptV^{\prime}\hskip 19.02095ptV}}}{{\hskip 8.23274pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle u,v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.81264pt\hskip 17.81264pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.23274pt\hskip-17.81264ptV^{\prime}\hskip 17.81264ptV}}}{}=\mathchoice{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\bigl\langle u,v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.1528pt\hskip 23.1528pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-23.1528ptU^{\prime}\hskip 23.1528ptU}}}{{\hskip 8.90308pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigl\langle u,v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.1528pt\hskip 23.1528pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-8.90308pt\hskip-23.1528ptU^{\prime}\hskip 23.1528ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bigl\langle u,v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-19.02095pt\hskip 19.02095pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-19.02095ptU^{\prime}\hskip 19.02095ptU}}}{{\hskip 7.96948pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigl\langle u,v\bigr\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.81264pt\hskip 17.81264pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-7.96948pt\hskip-17.81264ptU^{\prime}\hskip 17.81264ptU}}},\qquad u\in V,\;\;v\in U.

(2.33)

In view of (
2.20
), we infer that
the natural embedding
U\hookrightarrow H
is compact and thus, by duality we deduce we that

\mbox{the embedding }H\hookrightarrow U^{\prime}\mbox{ is compact}.

(2.34)

Remark 2.4
.

It is the last property of the space
U
, as well as (
2.36
), that we need in our paper, see the proof of Lemma
4.3
.

Finally, let us also observe that since the embedding
i:U\hookrightarrow H
is a contraction, so is the map
i^{\prime}:{H}\hookrightarrow U^{\prime}
.

We conclude this subsection with the introduction of two new spaces which will be needed in defining solutions
to the stochastic NSEs.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
B_{R}
be the open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
centered at
0
with radius
R>0
. We define the following spaces

H_{R}\coloneqq\{u\in H:\supp u\subset B_{R}\}\qquad V_{R}\coloneqq V\cap H_{R},

(2.35)

with the inner products and norms inherited respectively from the spaces
H
and
V
.

Since obviously
H_{R}\subset H
and
V_{R}\subset V
are closed subspaces and

\displaystyle H_{R}

\displaystyle\subset\bigl\{u\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{R}):\divergence u=0\bigr\},\;\;\;V_{R}\subset\bigl\{u\in\mathbb{H}^{1}(B_{R}):\divergence u=0\bigr\}.

Hence, since the sets
B_{R}
are bounded, for every
R>0
, by the Rellich-Kondratchov Theorem we infer that

the embedding
V_{R}\hookrightarrow H_{R}
is compact.

(2.36)

The symbols
H_{R}^{\prime}
and
V_{R}^{\prime}
will stand for the corresponding dual spaces.

2.3
The weak Stokes operator

In this subsection, we will introduce the (weak) Stokes operator
\mathscr{A}
and the bilinear operator
B
.

Since by the definition (
2.16
) of the norm in
V
,

|{\bigl(\nabla u,\nabla v\bigr)}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}|\leq\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.52437pt\hskip 17.52437pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.52437pt\hskip 17.52437pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.8046pt\hskip 15.8046pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.8046pt\hskip 15.8046pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|\nabla v|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.2059pt\hskip 25.2059pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.2059pt\hskip 25.2059pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|\nabla v|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.2059pt\hskip 25.2059pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.2059pt\hskip 25.2059pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|\nabla v|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.0475pt\hskip 17.0475pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.0475pt\hskip 17.0475pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|\nabla v|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.36473pt\hskip 15.36473pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.36473pt\hskip 15.36473pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}\leq\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.72447pt\hskip 25.72447pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.52437pt\hskip 17.52437pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.52437pt\hskip 17.52437pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|\nabla u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.8046pt\hskip 15.8046pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.8046pt\hskip 15.8046pt\mathbb{L}^{2}}}}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|v|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.20592pt\hskip 15.20592pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.20592pt\hskip 15.20592ptV}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|v|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.20592pt\hskip 15.20592pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.20592pt\hskip 15.20592ptV}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|v|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.00119pt\hskip 9.00119pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.00119pt\hskip 9.00119ptV}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|v|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.3739pt\hskip 8.3739pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.3739pt\hskip 8.3739ptV}}},\quad u,v\in V,

(2.37)

by the Riesz Lemma there exists a unique linear bounded operator

\mathscr{A}:V\to V^{\prime}

such that

\mathchoice{{\hskip 9.14935pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle\mathscr{A}u,v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-30.65282pt\hskip 30.65282pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.14935pt\hskip-30.65282ptV^{\prime}\hskip 30.65282ptV}}}{{\hskip 9.14935pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle\mathscr{A}u,v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-30.65282pt\hskip 30.65282pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.14935pt\hskip-30.65282ptV^{\prime}\hskip 30.65282ptV}}}{{\hskip 8.23274pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle\mathscr{A}u,v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-24.92377pt\hskip 24.92377pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.23274pt\hskip-24.92377ptV^{\prime}\hskip 24.92377ptV}}}{{\hskip 8.23274pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle\mathscr{A}u,v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-22.7155pt\hskip 22.7155pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.23274pt\hskip-22.7155ptV^{\prime}\hskip 22.7155ptV}}}{}={\bigl(\nabla u,\nabla v\bigr)}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},\qquad u,v\in V.

(2.38)

One can also prove that the operator
\mathscr{A}
, called here for obvious reasons the weak Stokes operator, is accretive.

2.4
The non-linear map

Next, let us consider the tri-linear form
b\colon V\times V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

b(u,w,v)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl(u\cdot\nabla w\bigr)\cdot v\,\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u_{i}\frac{\partial w_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}v_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x.

(2.39)

Because
V\subset\mathbb{L}^{6}
by the Sobolev Embedding Theorem, see
[
1
]
, the Lebesgue integral on the right-hand side of (
2.39
) exists and thus, by the Hölder and the Gagliardo-Ladyzhenskaya-Nirenberg inequalities, see
[
59
, Lemma III.3.3 and III.3.5]
we deduce that there exists a generic positive constant
c
such that the following estimates hold

\displaystyle|b(u,w,v)|\leq\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt\mathbb{L}^{4}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.47806pt\hskip 9.47806pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.47806pt\hskip 8.81377pt\mathbb{L}^{4}}}}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|\nabla v|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.2059pt\hskip 25.2059pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.2059pt\hskip \displaystyle\leq 2\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt\frac{1}{4}}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip (2.40)

\displaystyle\leq c\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|v|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.3739pt\hskip 8.3739pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.3739pt\hskip 8.3739ptV}}},\qquad u,w,v\in V.

In particular, we infer that the form
b
is continuous on
V
.

Let us also recall the following fundamental properties of the form
b
, see
[
59
, Lemma II.1.3]
,

b(u,w,v)=-b(u,v,w),\qquad u,w,v\in V

and

b(u,v,v)=0\qquad u,v\in V.

We also observe that by inequality (
2.40
) there exists a unique bounded, i.e. continuous, bilinear map

B\colon V\times V\to V^{\prime}

such that

\mathchoice{{\hskip 9.14935pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\big\langle B(u,w),v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-50.89008pt\hskip 50.89008pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.14935pt\hskip-50.89008ptV^{\prime}\hskip 50.89008ptV}}}{{\hskip 9.14935pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\big\langle B(u,w),v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-50.89008pt\hskip 50.89008pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip-9.14935pt\hskip-50.89008ptV^{\prime}\hskip 50.89008ptV}}}{{\hskip 8.23274pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\big\langle B(u,w),v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-39.89128pt\hskip 39.89128pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.23274pt\hskip-39.89128ptV^{\prime}\hskip 39.89128ptV}}}{{\hskip 8.23274pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\big\langle B(u,w),v\big\rangle}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-35.8412pt\hskip 35.8412pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-8.23274pt\hskip-35.8412ptV^{\prime}\hskip 35.8412ptV}}}{}\coloneqq b(u,w,v),\mbox{ for all }u,w,v\in V.

With a slight abuse of notation, we will also denote by
B
the corresponding quadratic map

B\colon V\ni u\mapsto B(u,u)\in V^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}.

By (
2.21
), there exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
u,w\in V
,
v\in V_{s}
,

\displaystyle|b(u,w,v)|=|b(u,v,w)|

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|u|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip 15.72449pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-15.72449pt\hskip
