\displaystyle\leq c\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-80.68639pt\hskip 80.68639pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-80.68639pt\hskip 80.68639ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-80.68639pt\hskip 80.68639pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-80.68639pt\hskip 80.68639ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-59.86458pt\hskip 59.86458pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-59.86458pt\hskip 59.86458ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-53.86452pt\hskip 53.86452pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-53.86452pt\hskip 53.86452ptH}}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|F(s,z_{n}(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-67.43402pt\hskip 67.43402pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-67.43402pt\hskip 67.43402ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|F(s,z_{n}(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-67.43402pt\hskip 67.43402pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-67.43402pt\hskip 67.43402ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|F(s,z_{n}(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-48.79288pt\hskip 48.79288pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-48.79288pt\hskip 48.79288ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|F(s,z_{n}(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.71568pt\hskip 43.71568pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.71568pt\hskip 43.71568ptH}}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\int_{0}^{T}(1+\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|z_{n}(s)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-32.99884pt\hskip 32.99884pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-32.99884pt\hskip 32.99884ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|z_{n}(s)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-32.99884pt\hskip 32.99884pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-32.99884pt\hskip 32.99884ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|z_{n}(s)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.78603pt\hskip 23.78603pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.78603pt\hskip 23.78603ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|z_{n}(s)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-21.88329pt\hskip 21.88329pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-21.88329pt\hskip 21.88329ptH}}})\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C_{2}(T+\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|z_{n}(s)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-32.99884pt\hskip 32.99884pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-32.99884pt\hskip 32.99884ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|z_{n}(s)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-32.99884pt\hskip 32.99884pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-32.99884pt\hskip 32.99884ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|z_{n}(s)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.78603pt\hskip 23.78603pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-23.78603pt\hskip 23.78603ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|z_{n}(s)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-21.88329pt\hskip 21.88329pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-21.88329pt\hskip 21.88329ptH}}}).

A similar inequality holds for the limit process
z
, i.e.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.88889pt\hskip 43.88889pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.88889pt\hskip 43.88889ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-38.81169pt\hskip 38.81169pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-38.81169pt\hskip 38.81169ptU^{\prime}}}}\mathds{1}_{D_{n}\cap D}(s,y)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq c\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.88889pt\hskip 43.88889pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.88889pt\hskip 43.88889ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-38.81169pt\hskip 38.81169pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-38.81169pt\hskip 38.81169ptH}}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\int_{0}^{T}(1+\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|z(s)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.55551pt\hskip 27.55551pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.55551pt\hskip 27.55551ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|z(s)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.55551pt\hskip 27.55551pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.55551pt\hskip 27.55551ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|z(s)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.88203pt\hskip 18.88203pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.88203pt\hskip 18.88203ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|z(s)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.9793pt\hskip 16.9793pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.9793pt\hskip 16.9793ptH}}})\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C_{2}(T+\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|z(s)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.55551pt\hskip 27.55551pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.55551pt\hskip 27.55551ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|z(s)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.55551pt\hskip 27.55551pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-27.55551pt\hskip 27.55551ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|z(s)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.88203pt\hskip 18.88203pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.88203pt\hskip 18.88203ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|z(s)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.9793pt\hskip 16.9793pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-16.9793pt\hskip 16.9793ptH}}}).

Moreover, by (
6.40
) we deduce the following estimate

I_{n}^{1}\leq C\left(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-140.45483pt\hskip 140.45483pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-140.45483pt\hskip 140.45483ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-140.45483pt\hskip 140.45483pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-140.45483pt\hskip 140.45483ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-101.69792pt\hskip 101.69792pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-101.69792pt\hskip 101.69792ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-90.45398pt\hskip 90.45398pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-90.45398pt\hskip 90.45398ptU^{\prime}}}}\mathds{1}_{D_{n}}(s,y)\mathds{1}_{D}(s,y)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}.

By the triangle inequality

\displaystyle I_{n}^{1}\leq 2C\biggl(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\biggl(

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-153.7072pt\hskip 153.7072pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-153.7072pt\hskip 153.7072ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-153.7072pt\hskip 153.7072pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-153.7072pt\hskip 153.7072ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-112.76962pt\hskip 112.76962pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-112.76962pt\hskip 112.76962ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-100.60281pt\hskip 100.60281pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-100.60281pt\hskip 100.60281ptU^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle+\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999ptU^{\prime}}}}\biggr)\mathds{1}_{D_{n}\cap D}(s,y)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\biggr)^{1/2}.

Next, by Lemma
2.6
(iii) we find that
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\lVert P_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathscr{L}(U^{\prime},U^{\prime})}\leq 1
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle I_{n}^{1}\leq

\displaystyle 2C\biggl(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-127.20247pt\hskip 127.20247pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-127.20247pt\hskip 127.20247ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-127.20247pt\hskip 127.20247pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-127.20247pt\hskip 127.20247ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-90.62622pt\hskip 90.62622pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-90.62622pt\hskip 90.62622ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-80.30515pt\hskip 80.30515pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-80.30515pt\hskip 80.30515ptU^{\prime}}}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s

(6.46)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999ptU^{\prime}}}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\biggr)^{1/2}.

By using Assumption F.
4

and (
6.37
) we see that the first term on the right hands side of (
6.46
) converges to 0 as
n\to\infty
. Regarding the second term, we argue as follows.
Since the embedding
H\hookrightarrow U^{\prime}
is continuous, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999ptU^{\prime}}}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999ptH}}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{aligned}

(6.47)

Next, we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999ptH}}}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-67.99329pt\hskip 67.99329pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-67.99329pt\hskip 67.99329ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-60.25577pt\hskip 60.25577pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-60.25577pt\hskip 60.25577ptH}}},

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-67.99329pt\hskip 67.99329pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-67.99329pt\hskip 67.99329ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-60.25577pt\hskip 60.25577pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-60.25577pt\hskip 60.25577ptH}}}

\displaystyle\searrow 0\mbox{ as }n\to\infty.

Finally, by (
6.40
)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-95.97906pt\hskip 95.97906ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-67.99329pt\hskip 67.99329pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-67.99329pt\hskip 67.99329ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|(I-P_{n})F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-60.25577pt\hskip 60.25577pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-60.25577pt\hskip 60.25577ptH}}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-61.99069pt\hskip 61.99069ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.88889pt\hskip 43.88889pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-43.88889pt\hskip 43.88889ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-38.81169pt\hskip 38.81169pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-38.81169pt\hskip 38.81169ptH}}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\leq C_{2}\int_{0}^{t}(1+\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|z(s))|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-31.44441pt\hskip 31.44441pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-31.44441pt\hskip 31.44441ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|z(s))|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-31.44441pt\hskip 31.44441pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-31.44441pt\hskip 31.44441ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|z(s))|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-22.00705pt\hskip 22.00705pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-22.00705pt\hskip 22.00705ptH}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|z(s))|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-19.68765pt\hskip 19.68765pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-19.68765pt\hskip 19.68765ptH}}})\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty.

Hence, by the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem we infer that

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-135.0115pt\hskip 135.0115ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-96.79393pt\hskip 96.79393ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-85.54999pt\hskip 85.54999ptU^{\prime}}}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\to 0\mbox{ as }n\to\infty.

Thus, we proved that the term
I_{n}^{1}
introduced in equality (
6.4
) satisfies

I_{n}^{1}\to 0\mbox{ as }n\to\infty.

(6.48)

Next, similarly to (
6.42
) we see that for
k\geq 2

\rho(P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y),A)>\frac{\alpha}{k}\implies a_{k}(P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y))=0.

Thus,

\displaystyle I_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\nu\left(y\in Y:(s,y)\in D_{n}\cap D^{c},\rho(P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y),A)\leq\frac{\alpha}{k}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\nu\left(y\in Y:\left\lvert\rho(P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y),A)-\rho(F(s,z(s),y),A)\right\rvert\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{k}\right)\alpha\right)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4k^{2}}{\alpha^{2}(k-2)^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\left\lvert\rho(P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y),A)-\rho(F(s,z(s),y),A)\right\rvert^{2}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s

By (
6.36
)

\displaystyle I_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4k^{2}}{\alpha^{2}(k-2)^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-140.45483pt\hskip 140.45483pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-140.45483pt\hskip 140.45483ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-140.45483pt\hskip 140.45483pt2}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-140.45483pt\hskip 140.45483ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-101.69792pt\hskip 101.69792pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-101.69792pt\hskip 101.69792ptU^{\prime}}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{|P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y)-F(s,z(s),y)|}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-90.45398pt\hskip 90.45398pt2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-90.45398pt\hskip 90.45398ptU^{\prime}}}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s.

With this estimate in mind we can follow the same reasoning as in the proof of assertion (
6.48
) and show that as
n\to\infty

I_{n}^{2}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}(P_{n}F(s,z_{n}(s),y))\mathds{1}_{D_{n}\cap D^{c}}(s,y)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\to 0.

Similarly, we also prove that as
n\to\infty

I_{n}^{3}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}(F(s,z(s),y))\mathds{1}_{D_{n}^{c}\cap D}(s,y)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\to 0.

Plugging the last two assertions and (
6.48
) into (
6.4
) implies (
6.39
).
This completes the proof of Lemma
6.22
.
∎

We now proceed with the statement and the proof of the following result.

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
z\in\mathscr{Z}_{T}
,
\alpha>0
and
A\in\mathscr{A}_{2}(\alpha)
.
Let
a_{k}
be defined by (
6.33
).

Then, for every pair
(s,t)\in[0,T]^{2}_{+}
,

\int_{s}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}(F(r,z(r),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}r\to\int_{s}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathds{1}_{A}(F(r,z(r),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}r.

(6.49)

Proof of Lemma
6.23
.

It is sufficient to assume that
s=0
.
Let us choose and fix
z\in\mathscr{Z}_{T}
,
\beta>\alpha>0
and

A\in\mathscr{A}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)
.
Then, thanks to Lemma
6.21
(i) we infer that

a_{k}(F(s,z(s),y))\searrow\mathds{1}_{A}(F(s,z(s),y))\mbox{ $\Leb\otimes\nu$-a.e.}.

(6.50)

Furthermore, since
z\in\mathscr{Z}_{T}
by using the linear growth condition (
3.5
) from Assumption F.
2

and the property (
6.34
) with
\ell=2
of the function
a_{2}
, we infer that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{2}(F(s,z(s),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C_{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\lVert F(s,z(s),y)\rVert^{2}_{U^{\prime}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{T}(1+\lVert z(s)\rVert^{2}_{H})\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty.

Thus, the convergence (
6.50
) and the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem imply (
6.49
) which completes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Lemma 6.24
.

Let us assume that
z\in\mathrm{D}([0,T];U^{\prime})
,
\alpha>0

and
A\in\mathscr{A}_{2}(\alpha)
.
Let
(a_{k})_{k\geq 2}
be a sequence of functions satisfying assumptions of Lemma
6.23
.

Then for every pair
(s,t)\in[0,T]^{2}_{+}
, the following convergence holds

\sum_{s<r\leq t}a_{k}(\Delta z(r))\to\sum_{s<r\leq t}\mathds{1}_{A}(\Delta z(r)).

(6.51)

Proof of Lemma
6.24
.

Let us choose and fix

z\in\mathrm{D}([0,T];U^{\prime})
,
\alpha>0

and
A\in\mathscr{A}_{2}(\alpha)
.
Since
z
is càdlàg there exists a finite set
r_{1}<\cdots<r_{N}\in[s,t]
at which
z
has jumps of size no smaller than
\frac{\alpha}{2}
. Hence, if
r\in[s,t]
is such that
a_{k}(\Delta z(r))\not=0
, then
r\in\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{N}\}
.
Therefore, from part (i) of Lemma
6.21

we infer that

\displaystyle\sum_{s<r\leq t}a_{k}(\Delta z(r))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{k}(\Delta z(r_{i}))\searrow\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathds{1}_{A}(\Delta z(r_{i}))=\sum_{s<r\leq t}\mathds{1}_{A}(\Delta z(r)).

The proof of Lemma
6.24
is complete.
∎

Lemma 6.25
.

Let
(a_{k})_{k\geq 2}
be the sequence defined in (
6.33
).
Then, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the real process
\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}
defined by

\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(t)=\sum\limits_{s\leq t}a_{k}(\Delta\overline{M}_{n}(s))-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}(P_{n}F(s,\overline{u}_{n}(s),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s,\;\;t\in[0,T],

(6.52)

is an
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
-martingale, where the filtration
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
was introduced in Definition
5.2
.

Proof of Lemma
6.25
.

Let us choose and fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 2
.
Let us define an auxiliary real process
{N}_{a_{k},n}
by

{N}_{a_{k},n}(t)=\sum\limits_{s\leq t}a_{k}(\Delta{M}_{n}(s))-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}(P_{n}F(s,u_{n}(s),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s,\;\;t\in[0,T].

(6.53)

Notice that by Remark
6.18
and by
[
39
, Corollary 2.4.2]
, the first terms on the right-hand sides of identities (
6.53
) and (
6.52
) are
\mathbb{R}
-valued,
{\mathbb{F}}
-, respectively
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{n}}
-optional, càdlàg processes.

To prove that the process
\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}
is a
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
-martingale we argue as follows.
We first notice that because the jumps of the processes
\overline{M}_{n}
, resp.
{M}_{n}
, are the same as the jumps of the processes
\overline{u}_{n}
, resp.
u_{n}
, see equality (
5.8
), we have that on
\overline{\Omega}

\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(t)=\sum\limits_{s\leq t}a_{k}(\Delta\overline{u}_{n}(s))-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}(P_{n}F(s,\overline{u}_{n}(s),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s,\;\;t\in[0,T],

and on
\Omega
,

{N}_{a_{k},n}(t)=\sum\limits_{s\leq t}a_{k}(\Delta{u}_{n}(s))-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}(P_{n}F(s,u_{n}(s),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s,\;\;t\in[0,T].

Next, by identity (
6.19
) in Lemma
6.15
applied to the function
g=a_{k}
we infer that
every
t\in[0,T]
, the following identity hold
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely,

\displaystyle{N}_{a_{k},n}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}\bigl(P_{n}F(s,{u}_{n}(s),y)\bigr)\tilde{{\eta}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y),\;\;t\geq 0.

(6.54)

Thus, after noting that the integrand belongs to the space
\mathbf{F}_{p}^{2}
with
p
equal to the Poisson point process corresponding to the Poisson random measure
\eta
, we infer that
N_{a_{k},n}
is an
\mathbb{F}
-martingale, see
[
15
, Section 3]
.

Now let us consider the map
\Psi_{a_{k},n}\colon\mathscr{Z}_{T}\to\mathbb{D}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
defined by

\left(\Psi_{a_{k},n}(u)\right)(t)=\sum_{s\in[0,t]}a_{k}(\Delta u(s))-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}\left(P_{n}F(s,u(s),y)\right)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s

(6.55)

for
u\in\mathscr{Z}_{T}
and
t\in[0,T]
.

Thanks to assertion (
6.38
) in Lemma
6.22
and
[
39
, Corollary 2.4.2]
, we infer that the map
\Psi_{a_{k},n}
is measurable. This, along with the measurability of the evaluation map
\pi_{t}\colon\mathbb{D}([0,T];\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
, see proof of
[
41
, Lemma 4]
and a related result in
[
4
, Theorem 12.5(ii)]
, implies that the function
\pi_{t}\circ\Psi_{a_{k},n}
is measurable for all
t\in[0,T]
. Thus, by the martingale property of
N_{a_{k},n}
and the equality of laws of
\overline{u}_{n}
and
u_{n}
on
\mathscr{Z}_{T}
we deduce that for every pair
(s,t)\in[0,T]^{2}_{+}
and
every continuous and bounded function
h\colon\mathbb{D}([0,s];U^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R}

the following equality holds

\mathbb{E}\left[h(u_{n}{\lvert_{[0,s]}})\left(N_{a_{k},n}(t)-N_{a_{k},n}(s)\right)\right]=\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[h(\overline{u}_{n}{\lvert_{[0,s]}})\left(\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(t)-\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(s)\right)\right]=0.

(6.56)

Hence, since
h
,
s
and
t
are arbitrary, we infer that the process
\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}
is a martingale with respect to the filtration generated by
\overline{u}_{n}
.
Since by Proposition
5.3
, the filtration
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{n}
is equal to the usual augmentation of the
filtration
\mathbb{H}_{n}
generated by
\overline{u}_{n}
(see Definition
5.2
), in view of Lemma
E.2
, the proof of Lemma
6.25
is complete.
∎

Note that according to Remark
6.18
the process
\overline{N}_{A}
is well defined.

According to our next result, whose proof will be given at the end of this subsection, in order to prove Proposition
6.20
it is sufficient to prove it for a certain smaller class of sets
A
.

Lemma 6.26
.

If the process
\overline{N}_{A}
defined by (
6.32
) is an
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
-martingale for every set
A\in\sigma\bigl(\mathscr{A}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)\bigr)
for some
\beta>\alpha>0
,
then the process
\overline{N}_{A}
is an
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
-martingale for every
set
A\in\mathscr{A}_{0}
.

The proof of this lemma requires several lemmata and is postponed to page
6.4
.
We will also prove the following companion result.

Lemma 6.27
.

Let
A\in\mathscr{A}_{1}
, i.e.
A\in\mathscr{A}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)
for some
\beta>\alpha>0
.
Then, the process
\overline{N}_{A}
defined by
(
6.32
)
is an
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
-martingale.

Lemma
6.27
will also be proved later, but for now let us formulate a corollary to it. Note that

\mathscr{B}(S_{\alpha,\beta})=\sigma\bigl(\mathscr{A}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)\bigr)
.

Corollary 6.28
.

If
A\in\mathscr{B}(S_{\alpha,\beta})
for some
\beta>\alpha>0
,
then the process
\overline{N}_{A}
defined by
(
6.32
)
is an
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
-martingale.

Now, we will formulate the following lemma where therein the filtration
\mathbb{H}=(\mathscr{H}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is the filtration generated by
\overline{u}
, see (
5.26
).

Lemma 6.29
.

Let
C
be the set defined in (
5.32
). Let
\overline{N}_{a_{k}}=\left(\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t):t\geq 0\right)
be the
\mathbb{R}
-valued process defined by

\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)\coloneqq\sum\limits_{s\leq t}a_{k}(\Delta\overline{M}(s))-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}(F(s,\overline{u}(s),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s,\;\;t\geq 0.

(6.57)

Then for every pair
(s,t)\in C^{2}_{+}

\overline{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)\lvert\mathscr{H}_{s}\bigr]=\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s).

(6.58)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\sup_{k\geq 2}\;\;\sup_{t\in C}\;\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lvert{\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)}\rvert^{2}\right]<\infty.

(6.59)

Proof of Lemma
6.29
.

First we will deal with the proof of assertion (
6.58
). For this aim we closely follow
[
35
]
and denote by
\mathscr{D}_{t}^{0}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by all maps

\mathbb{D}([0,T],U^{\prime})\ni x\mapsto x(s)\in U^{\prime},\,s\leq t.

We also set
\mathscr{D}_{t}=\bigcap_{s>t}\mathscr{D}^{0}_{s},\,t\in[0,T]
. Note that
\mathbf{D}=(\mathscr{D}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a filtration. Next, let us choose and fix a pair
(s,t)\in C^{2}_{+}
and a continuous and bounded function
h\colon\mathbb{D}([0,T];U^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R}
such that
h
is
\mathscr{D}_{s}
-measurable. By using standard Functional Monotone Class argument as in
[
35
, Proposition IX.1.1]
, to prove (
6.58
) it is enough to prove that

\overline{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[(\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)-\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s))h(\overline{u})\bigr]=0.

(6.60)

Let us define, compare with (
6.55
), a map
\Psi_{a_{k}}\colon\mathscr{Z}_{T}\to\mathbb{D}([0,T];\mathbb{R})
by

\left(\Psi_{a_{k}}(u)\right)(t)=\sum_{s\in[0,t]}a_{k}(\Delta u(s))-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}\left(F(s,u(s),y)\right)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s.

We first claim that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Psi_{a_{k},n}(\overline{u}_{n})(t)-\Psi_{a_{k}}(\overline{u})(t)=0\quad\mbox{ on $\bar{\Omega}$. }

We will separately show the convergence of the first and the second terms defining the functions
\Psi_{a_{k},n}(\overline{u}_{n})
and
\Psi_{a_{k}}(\overline{u})
.

Since the evaluation map
\pi_{t}\colon\mathbb{D}([0,T];U^{\prime})\ni x\mapsto x(t)\in U^{\prime}
is continuous if and only if
x
is continuous at
t
, the convergence of the second terms follows from the definition of the set
C
, the convergence (
4.5
)
according to which
{\overline{u}}_{n}\to\overline{u}
in
\mathscr{Z}_{T}
on
\overline{\Omega}
and
[
39
, Corollary 2.4.2]
.
The convergence of the first terms follows from Lemma
6.22
.

Let us also notice that
\lim_{n\to\infty}h({\overline{u}}_{n})=h(\overline{u})
on
\overline{\Omega}
. Hence, we also have on
\overline{\Omega}

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(t)-\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(s)\right)h(\overline{u}_{n})=\left(\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)-\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s)\right)h(\overline{u}).

(6.61)

We now prove the uniform integrability of the random variables

\left(\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(t)-\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(s)\right)h(\overline{u}_{n}),\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Obviously it is sufficient to prove the following stronger property

\displaystyle C_{4}\coloneqq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lvert{\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(r)}\rvert^{2}\right]<\infty.

(6.62)

In order to prove (
6.62
)
we observe first that by the measurability of
\pi_{t}\circ\Psi_{a_{k},n}
and the equality of laws of
{\overline{u}}_{n}
and
u_{n}
on
\mathscr{Z}_{T}

\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lvert{\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(r)}\rvert^{2}\right]=\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert{N_{a_{k},n}(r)}\rvert^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\lvert{N_{a_{k},n}(r)}\rvert^{2}\right].

Next, by the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality applied to the real-valued process
{N}_{a_{k},n}
satisfying equality (
6.54
), see
[
38
, Theorem 26.12]
,
there exists a constant
c_{3}>0

such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\geq 2

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\lvert{N_{a_{k},n}(r)}\rvert^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{Y}\left(a_{k}(F_{n}(s,u_{n}(s),y))\right)^{2}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\frac{2^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{Y}\left\lVert P_{n}F(s,u_{n}(s),y)\right\rVert_{U^{\prime}}^{2}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{Y}\left\lVert P_{n}F(s,u_{n}(s),y)\right\rVert_{H}^{2}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\right],

where the second inequality is a consequence of the condition (
6.34
) with
\ell=1
and the last
inequality is a consequence of the boundedness of the natural embedding
H\hookrightarrow U^{\prime}
.

Since
P_{n}\colon H\to H
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, is an orthogonal projection, see (
2.49
), by the linear growth condition (
3.5
) in Assumption F.
2

and the estimate (
4.3
) with
p=2
we deduce that there exists a constant
c_{4}>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
k\geq 2

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{Y}\left\lVert P_{n}F(s,u_{n}(s),y)\right\rVert_{H}^{2}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[c_{4}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{Y}\left\lVert F(s,u_{n}(s),y)\right\rVert_{H}^{2}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\left(1+\left\lVert u_{n}(s)\right\rVert_{H}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\right]<\infty.

(6.63)

All the previous inequalities together with the a’ priori estimate (
4.3
) with
p=2
from Lemma
4.2
imply the sought estimate (
6.62
).

Now, by the Vitali Convergence Theorem applied to the almost sure convergence (
6.61
) we infer that (
6.60
), and hence (
6.58
), holds.

Finally, we will deal with the proof of inequality (
6.59
). Let us choose and fix
k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
and
t\in C
. Then by (
6.61
), the Fatou Lemma and inequality (
6.62
) we have the following chain of inequalities

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lvert{\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)}\rvert^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lvert{\lim_{n\to\infty}\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(t)}\rvert^{2}\right]=\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lim_{n\to\infty}\lvert{\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(t)}\rvert^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lvert{\overline{N}_{a_{k},n}(t)}\rvert^{2}\right]\leq C_{4}.

Note that the finiteness follows from Assumption Assumption F.
2
.
This obviously implies inequality (
6.59
) and hence, the proof of Lemma
6.29
is complete.
∎

Lemma 6.30
.

In the framework of Lemma
6.29
, the process

\overline{N}_{a_{k}}
is an
\overline{\mathbb{F}}
-martingale.

Proof of Lemma
6.30
.

Let us choose and fix a pair
(s,t)\in[0,T]^{2}_{+}
such that
s<t
. We consider an
C^{2}_{+}
-valued sequence
(s_{m},t_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying

•

s<s_{m}<t<t_{m}
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

s_{m}\searrow s
and
t_{m}\searrow t
as
m\to\infty
.

Let
\mathbf{D}=(\mathscr{D}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be the filtration defined in the proof of Lemma
6.29
. We also fix an arbitrary
h:\mathbb{D}([0,T];U^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R}
bounded, continuous and
\mathscr{D}_{s}
-measurable function.
Observe that by Lemma
6.29
and its proof, see equality (
6.60
) and inequality (
6.59
), we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\left(\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t_{m})-\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s_{m})\right)h(\overline{u})\bigr]=0,\qquad\mbox{ for all }m\in\mathbb{N},

(6.64)

\displaystyle\sup_{k\geq 2}\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lvert{\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t_{m})}\rvert^{2}\right]<\infty.

(6.65)

The second property above and the boundedness of
h
imply that the sequences
(\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t_{m}))_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s_{m}))_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\left(\bigl[\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t_{m})-\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s_{m})\bigr]\right)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly integrable. Thus, we can proceed as in Step 3 of the proof of Lemma
5.10
and prove that

\displaystyle\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s_{m})\to\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s)\mbox{ in }L^{1}(\overline{\Omega}),

(6.66)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\left(\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)-\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s)\right)h(\overline{u})\bigr]=\lim_{m\to\infty}\overline{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\left(\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t_{m})-\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s_{m})\right)h(\overline{u})\bigr]=0.

The second set of equations along with a standard Functional Monotone Class argument imply that

\overline{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)\lvert\mathscr{H}_{s}\bigr]=\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s).

Since the filtration
\overline{\mathscr{F}}=(\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is the usual augmentation of the filtration
(\mathscr{H}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
generated by
\overline{u}
, we infer from Lemma
E.2
that
\overline{N}_{a_{k}}
is an
\bar{\mathbb{F}}
-martingale. This completes of the proof of Lemma
6.30
.
∎

We now give the promised proof of Lemma
6.27
.

Proof of Lemma
6.27
.

Let us choose and fix
A\in\mathscr{A}_{2}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha>0
. Let us point out that this set satisfies assumptions of Lemmata
6.23
and
6.24
.
Next, let us choose and fix a pair
(s,t)\in[0,T]^{2}_{+}
. Let us recall that the process
{\overline{N}}_{A}
has been defined in equality (
6.32
).

Let us observe that by equality (
6.49
) from Lemma
6.23
and by equality (
6.51
) from Lemma
6.24

we have respectively the following two assertions
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\int_{Y}a_{k}(F(r,\overline{u}(r),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}r\to\int_{s}^{t}\int_{Y}1_{A}(F(r,\overline{u}(r),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}r,

\displaystyle\sum_{s\leq r\leq t}a_{k}(\Delta\overline{M}(r))\to\sum_{s\leq r\leq t}\mathds{1}_{A}(\Delta\overline{M}(r))\mbox{ as }k\to\infty.

Therefore, in view of definition (
6.32
) of the process
\overline{N}_{A}
and definition (
6.57
) of the process
\overline{N}_{a_{k}}
we infer that

\overline{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.

\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)-\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s)\to\overline{N}_{A}(t)-\overline{N}_{A}(s)\mbox{ as }k\to\infty.

Let us observe that by a similar argument we used to prove inequality (
6.59
) in Lemma
6.29
, and by using
(
6.59
), (
6.65
) and (
6.66
) we can deduce that

\sup_{k\geq 2}\overline{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\lvert\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s)\rvert^{2}+\overline{\mathbb{E}}\lvert\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)\rvert^{2}\Bigr]<\infty.

This implies that the sequence
\rvert\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)\rvert
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, is uniformly integrable and therefore by the Vitali Convergence Theorem we infer that

\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)\to\overline{N}_{A}(t)\mbox{ in $L^{1}(\overline{\Omega})$ as }k\to\infty.

This along with Lemma
6.30
according to which

\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left[\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)-\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s)\right]\bigg|\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\right)=0,

we deduce that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left[\overline{N}_{A}(t)-\overline{N}_{A}(s)\right]\biggl|\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\right)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left[\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(t)-\overline{N}_{a_{k}}(s)\right]\biggl|\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\right)=0\mbox{ in $L^{1}(\overline{\Omega})$}.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
6.27
.
∎

The following proof provides some details to the 5 lines long proof of
[
39
, Lemma 6.1.11]
.
Since we have not been able to follow that proof we have decided to provide a detailed proof as below. Our proof refers to Lemma
6.29
which was formulated and proved earlier on.

Proof of Corollary
6.28
.

Let us choose and fix
\beta>\alpha>0
and
t\geq s\geq 0
. Let
A\in\sigma(\mathscr{A}_{1}(\alpha,\beta))
. We shall prove that

\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{N}_{A}(t)\lvert\bar{\mathscr{F}_{s}}\right]=\overline{N}_{A}(s).

For this purpose let us consider the family

\mathscr{B}_{\alpha,\beta}=\{B\in\mathscr{B}(S_{\alpha,\beta}):\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{N}_{B}(t)\lvert\bar{\mathscr{F}_{s}}\right]=\overline{N}_{B}(s),\,\mbox{ $\overline{\mathbb{P}}$-a.s.}\}.

Note that if
B\in\mathscr{B}(S_{\alpha,\beta})
is closed, then it follows from the proof of Lemma
6.27
that
\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{N}_{B}(t)\lvert\bar{\mathscr{F}_{s}}\right]
is well-defined. If
B\in\mathscr{B}(S_{\alpha,\beta})
is open, then there exists a closed (the trace of a Borel closed set in
U^{\prime}
on
S_{\alpha,\beta}
)
C\in\mathscr{B}_{\alpha,\beta}
such that
B=S_{\alpha,\beta}\setminus C
and, by the proof of Lemma
6.27
, we see that
\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{N}_{B}\right]=\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{N}_{S_{\alpha,\beta}}\right]-\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{N}_{C}\right]<\infty
, which implies that
\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{N}_{B}(t)\lvert\bar{\mathscr{F}_{s}}\right]
is well-defined.

Next, it is not difficult to see that
\mathscr{A}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)
is a
\pi
-system, i.e., it is closed under the formation of finite intersections. We also have the following:

(i)

S_{\alpha,\beta}\in\mathscr{B}_{\alpha,\beta}
. In fact this follows from the fact that
S_{\alpha,\beta}\in\mathscr{A}_{\alpha,\beta}
and Lemma
6.27
.

(ii)

If
A,B\in\mathscr{B}_{\alpha,\beta}
with
A\subset B
, then
B\setminus A\in\mathscr{B}_{\alpha,\beta}
. In fact, since
A\subset B
, we have
1_{B\setminus A}=\mathds{1}_{B}-\mathds{1}_{A}
and
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.

\displaystyle\overline{N}_{B\setminus A}=

\displaystyle\overline{N}_{B}-\overline{N}_{A},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{N}_{B\setminus A}(t)\lvert\bar{\mathscr{F}_{s}}]=

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}[(\overline{N}_{B}(t)-\overline{N}_{A}(t))\lvert\bar{\mathscr{F}_{s}}]=\overline{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{N}_{B}(t)\lvert\bar{\mathscr{F}_{s}}]-\overline{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{N}_{A}(t)\lvert\bar{\mathscr{F}_{s}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{N}_{B}(s)-\overline{N}_{A}(s)=\overline{N}_{B\setminus A}(s).

(iii)

If
(B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{B}_{\alpha,\beta},\mbox{ with }B_{n}\subset B_{n+1}\mbox{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}
,
then
B\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\geq 1}B_{n}\in\mathscr{B}_{\alpha,\beta}
. In fact, it follows from the Monotone Convergence Theorem that
\displaystyle\overline{N}_{B_{n}}(r)\nearrow\overline{N}_{B}(r)
on
\overline{\Omega}
for any
r\in[0,T]
and
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{N}_{B}(t)\lvert\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{s}]

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathbb{E}}[\lim_{n\to\infty}\overline{N}_{B_{n}}(t)\lvert\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{s}]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\overline{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{N}_{B_{n}}(t)\lvert\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{s}]

(6.67)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\overline{N}_{B_{n}}(s)=\overline{N}_{B}(s).

From the observations (i)-(iii) we infer that
\mathscr{B}_{\alpha,\beta}
is a
\lambda
-system, which along with
[
5
, Theorem I.3.2]
implies that
\sigma\bigl(\mathscr{A}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)\bigr)\subset\mathscr{B}_{\alpha,\beta}
. Thus, from this last result and the definition of
\mathscr{B}_{\alpha,\beta}
, we easily complete the proof of the Corollary
6.28
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.26
.

Let us choose and fix an arbitrary
A\in\mathscr{A}_{0}
, i.e., a Borel set
A\subset U^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
for which (
6.31
) holds. Let us define a sequence
(A_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
of Borel sets by

A_{n}\coloneqq A\cap S_{1/n,n}.

Let us choose and fix
s<t\in[0,T]
. Because
A_{n}
is a Borel subset of
S_{1/n,n}
, it follows from
Corollary
6.28
that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely,

\overline{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{N}_{A_{n}}(t)|\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{s}]=\overline{N}_{A_{n}}(s).

(6.68)

Observe that
\displaystyle\overline{N}_{A_{n}}(r)\nearrow\overline{N}_{A}(r)
on
\overline{\Omega}
for any
r\in[0,T]
. Thus, from (
6.68
) and the Monotone Convergence Theorem we deduce that
\overline{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely

\overline{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{N}_{A}(t)|\bar{\mathscr{F}}_{s}]=\overline{N}_{A}(s).

(6.69)

Because the pair
(s,t)\in[0,T]^{2}_{+}
is arbitrary, this completes the proof of Lemma
6.26
.
∎

End of the proof of Proposition
6.20
.

As explained earlier,
Proposition
6.20
follows from Lemma
6.26
.
∎

6.5
Applying the Martingale Representation Theorem

Now, we are ready to complete the proof of the main result of our paper, see Theorem
3.11
.

Proof of Theorem
3.11
.

We will now apply Theorem
D.6
about the representation theorem of purely discontinuous martingales in terms of a Poisson random measure. For this aim we will verify all assumptions
of that theorem. We begin with fixing the notation. We put

-

\mathbf{X}=U^{\prime}
, where
U
is the Hilbert space introduced in (
2.23
);

-

as the filtered probability space
\mathfrak{A}
we consider
the probability space
\bigl(\overline{\Omega},\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
introduced at the beginning of subsection
4.3
, endowed with the filtration
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}\coloneqq({\overline{\mathscr{F}}}_{t})
defined in equality (
5.26
);

-

as the martingale
M
we consider the process
\overline{M}
defined in equation (
5.27
);

-

as the space
(Y,\mathscr{Y})
we take the space
(Y,\mathscr{Y})
from Assumption F.
1
;

-

as the map
\theta
we consider the following function

\theta\colon[0,\infty)\times Y\times\overline{\Omega}\ni(t,y,\overline{\omega})\mapsto F(t,\overline{u}(t-,\overline{\omega}),y)\in U^{\prime},

(6.70)

where the function
F
is from Assumption F.
2
and
\overline{u}
is the
\mathscr{Z}_{T}
-valued random variable introduced at the beginning of subsection
4.3
.

-

As the measure
m
we take the measure
\nu
from Assumption
1
.

Note that with this choice of function
\theta
, assumption (
D.12
)
is satisfied. Indeed,

\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{Y}\left\lVert F(t,\overline{u}(t-),y)\right\rVert_{U^{\prime}}^{2}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\leq C_{2}\overline{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(1+\left\lVert\overline{u}(t)\right\rVert_{H}^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty

(6.71)

by Assumption F.
2
and assertion (
4.10
).

Obviously, our space
\mathbf{X}
is a separable Banach space. According to Assumption F.
1
,
(Y,\mathscr{Y})
is a standard measurable space.

According to Lemma
5.10
the process
{\overline{M}}
is a square integrable,
U^{\prime}
-valued càdlàg
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
-martingale.

Moreover, the martingale
{\overline{M}}
is purely discontinuous, see Proposition
6.12
.

Since
\overline{u}
is a
\mathscr{Z}_{T}
-valued random variable, all the trajectories of the corresponding process
\overline{u}(t)
,
t\in[0,T]
, are
U^{\prime}
-valued càdlàg. Hence, since the process
\overline{u}
is
also
\overline{\mathbb{F}}
-adapted, its left limit version
\overline{u}(t-)
,
t\in[0,T]
, is a
U^{\prime}
-valued
\overline{\mathbb{F}}
-predictable process. Therefore, by the measurability property of the function
F
from Assumption F.
1
, the generalized process

F(t,\overline{u}(t-,\overline{\omega}),y)
,
(t,\overline{\omega},y)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times Y
, is
\overline{\mathbb{F}}
-predictable, see
[
34
, Definition II.3.3]
.
This implies that the function
\theta
is
\overline{\mathbb{F}}
-predictable.

Next, let
\eta_{\overline{M}}
on
U^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
be the integer-valued random measure defined by

\eta_{\overline{M}}(t,A;\overline{\omega})=\sum_{s\in(0,t]}\mathds{1}_{A}(\Delta\overline{M}(s;\overline{\omega})),\;\;t>0,\;A\in\mathscr{B}(U^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}),\overline{\omega}\in\overline{\Omega}.

We will show that
\eta_{\overline{M}}
is a random measure of class (QL), see Definition
2.3
.

For this purpose let us choose and fix a set
A\in\mathscr{B}(U^{\prime}\setminus\{0\})
satisfying (
6.31
). Then we claim that by Proposition
6.20

the process
\tilde{\eta}_{\overline{M}}(t,A)
,
t\geq 0
,
defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}_{\overline{M}}(t,A)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\eta_{\overline{M}}(t,A)-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathds{1}_{A}(F(s,\overline{u}(s-),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s

(6.72)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in(0,t]}\mathds{1}_{A}(\Delta\overline{M}(s))-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}\mathds{1}_{A}(F(s,\overline{u}(s),y))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathrm{d}s,\;\;t\geq 0,

is an
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}
-martingale. We conclude that the compensator
\hat{\eta}_{\overline{M}}
of
\eta_{\overline{M}}
is given by

\hat{\eta}_{\overline{M}}(t,A)=\int_{0}^{t}\nu\left(y\in Y:F(t,\overline{u}(s),y)\in A\right)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Inside equality (
6.72
) we can freely replace
\overline{u}(s-)
with
\overline{u}(s)
because the functions
(0,t]\ni s\mapsto\overline{u}(s-)\in U^{\prime}
and
(0,t]\ni s\mapsto\overline{u}(s)\in U^{\prime}
differ at a most countable set, as the latter function is càdlàg, so that their Lebesgue integrals are equal.
Therefore we conclude that the random measure
\eta_{\overline{M}}
is a of class (QL) and that assumption (
D.13
) holds.

We conclude by applying Theorem
D.6
according to which there exists an extension
(\underline{\Omega},\underline{\mathscr{F}},\underline{\mathbb{P}},\underline{\mathbb{F}})
of
(\overline{\Omega},\overline{\mathscr{F}},\overline{\mathbb{P}},\overline{\mathbb{F}})
and a Poisson random measure
\underline{\eta}
with characteristic measure
\nu
such that for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\overline{M}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{Y}F(s,\overline{u}(s-,\underline{\omega}),y)\tilde{\underline{\eta}}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}y)\mbox{ in }U^{\prime},\underline{\mathbb{P}}\mbox{-almost surely}.

(6.73)

Note that
\overline{u}
trivially extends to a process
\underline{u}
defined on
\underline{\Omega}
.
Substituting (
6.73
) into (
5.27
) we see that
(\underline{\Omega},\underline{\mathscr{F}},\underline{\mathbb{P}},\underline{\mathscr{F}}_{t},\underline{u},\underline{\eta})
is a solution of the Navier-Stokes equation.

The proof of Theorem
3.11
is thus complete.
∎

Appendix A
The Itô formula

Itô formula play an important role in this paper. In this short section we recall the following special case of the Itô Theorem, see
[
35
, Theorem I.4.57]
.

Theorem A.1
.

Suppose that
\xi
is real-valued semimartingale. Then for every
t\geq 0
, almost surely

\displaystyle\xi(t)^{2}

\displaystyle=\xi(0)^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}2\xi(s-)\,\mathrm{d}\xi(s)+\langle\xi^{\mathrm{c}}\rangle(t)+\sum_{s\leq t}(\Delta\xi(s))^{2},

(A.1)

\displaystyle=\xi(0)^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}2\xi(s-)\,\mathrm{d}\xi(s)+[\xi](t).

(A.2)

Proof.

The theorem is a direct corollary from
[
35
, Theorem I.4.57]
. The fact that (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) are equal follows from
[
35
, Theorem I.4.52]
.
∎

This special case of the Ito formula is used in the proof of Proposition
6.11
.

Appendix B
An extension of a probability space

We begin with recalling
[
34
, Definition II.7.1, p. 89]
. We recommend the reader to read also Definition II.7.2 from the same source.

Remark B.1
.

Let us note that when we write
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{U}}\cdot\eta(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u)
, then the value of the point process
p
corresponding to
\eta
at
0
does not play any role, whereas its value at
t
does influence the integral. For this reason some authors, see for instance
[
34
]
, write
\int_{0}^{t+}\int_{\mathbb{U}}\cdot\eta(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u)
or
\int_{(0,t]}\int_{\mathbb{U}}\cdot\eta(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u)
.

Definition B.2
.

We say that a system
\bigl(\tilde{\mathfrak{A}},\pi\bigr)
,
where

\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}=\bigl(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathscr{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}}\bigr)

is a filtered probability space, with
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}=(\tilde{\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, and
\pi\colon\tilde{\Omega}\to\Omega

is a map, is an extension of a
filtered probability space

\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}},{\mathbb{F}}\bigr),\mbox{ where }{\mathbb{F}}=({\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0},

if and only if the following four conditions are satisfied

\pi
is
\tilde{\mathscr{F}}/{\mathscr{F}}
-measurable;

\pi
is
\tilde{\mathscr{F}}_{t}/{\mathscr{F}}_{t}
-measurable for every
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\circ\pi^{-1}
,

for every
{\mathscr{F}}
-measurable and
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. bounded, function
X\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, and every
t\geq 0
, the following equality holds

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\tilde{X}|\tilde{\mathscr{F}}_{t}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[{X}|{\mathscr{F}}_{t}\bigr]\circ\pi,\;\;\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\mbox{-a.s.}

where

\tilde{X}\coloneqq X\circ\pi\;\;\;:\tilde{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}.

The notion of predictability used in the following obvious result was defined in
[
34
, Definition II.3.3]
, see also
[
39
, Definition 3.4.4]
.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
(\mathbf{X},\mathscr{B}_{\mathbf{X}})
and
(\mathbf{U},\mathcal{U})
be two measurable spaces.
If

\theta\colon(0,\infty)\times\mathbf{U}\times\Omega\to\mathbf{X}

is a generalized predictable process, then
the process
\tilde{\theta}
defined by

\tilde{\theta}\colon(0,\infty)\times\mathbf{U}\times\tilde{\Omega}\ni(t,u,\tilde{\omega})\mapsto\theta\bigl(t,u,\pi(\tilde{\omega})\bigr)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathbf{X}}

(B.1)

is a generalized predictable process, see
[
34
, Definition II.3.3]
.

Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

Let
\tilde{\mathscr{P}}_{\mathbf{U}}
denote the predictable
\sigma
-field on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbf{U}\times\tilde{\Omega}
. It is sufficient to show that the map

(\mathrm{id}_{(0,\infty)},\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{U}},\pi)\colon(0,\infty)\times\mathbf{U}\times\tilde{\Omega}\ni(t,u,\tilde{\omega})\mapsto\bigl(t,u,\pi(\tilde{\omega})\bigr)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbf{U}\times\Omega

is
\tilde{\mathscr{P}}_{\mathbf{U}}/\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{U}}
measurable.
For this purpose, we identify the set
(0,\infty)\times\mathbf{U}\times\Omega
with
(0,\infty)\times\Omega\times\mathbf{U}
. With this in mind we see that

\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{U}}=\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathcal{U}
and
\tilde{\mathscr{P}}_{\mathbf{U}}=\tilde{\mathscr{P}}\otimes\mathcal{U}
, where
\tilde{\mathscr{P}}
(resp.
\mathscr{P}
) is the predictable
\sigma
-algebra on
(0,\infty)\times\tilde{\Omega}
(resp.
(0,\infty)\times\Omega
).
Thus, to prove the lemma it is enough to show that
(\mathrm{id}_{(0,\infty)},\pi)
is
\tilde{\mathscr{P}}/\mathscr{P}
-measurable. So, let
s\leq t
,
F\in\mathscr{F}_{s}
and
F_{0}\in\mathscr{F}_{0}
. Since
\pi
is
\tilde{\mathscr{F}}_{r}/\mathscr{F}_{r}
-measurable for any
r\geq 0
, we have
(\mathrm{id}_{(0,\infty)},\pi)^{-1}((s,t]\times{F})\in\tilde{\mathscr{P}}
and
(\mathrm{id}_{(0,\infty)},\pi)^{-1}(\{0\}\times{F}_{0})\in\tilde{\mathscr{P}}
.
Since the
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathscr{P}}
is generated by the sets
(s,t]\times{F}
and
\{0\}\times{F}_{0}
with
s\leq t
and
{F}\in{\mathscr{F}}_{s}
and
F_{0}\in{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
, see
[
45
, Theorem 3.3]
, we have proved that
(\mathrm{id}_{(0,\infty)},\pi)
is
\tilde{\mathscr{P}}/\mathscr{P}
-measurable. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We also have the following, which shows the importance of property (iv) in Definition
B.2
.

Proposition B.4
.

Assume that
M=(M_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a
p
-integrable, for some
p\in(1,\infty)
, martingale on a
filtered probability space

\mathfrak{A}=\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}},{\mathbb{F}}\bigr),\mbox{ where }{\mathbb{F}}=({\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
.
Assume that a filtered probability space

\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}=\bigl(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathscr{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}}\bigr)
, where
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}=(\tilde{\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
,
is an extension of the former. Define a process
\tilde{M}=(\tilde{M}(t))_{t\geq 0}
by

\tilde{M}(t)\coloneqq M(t)\circ\pi\;\;\;(:\tilde{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}),

(B.2)

where
\pi\colon\tilde{\Omega}\to\Omega
is a map as in Definition
B.2
. Then

\tilde{M}
is a martingale on
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Proof.

The integrability condition is obvious. Indeed, by property (iii) in Definition
B.2
the change of measure theorem we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[|\tilde{M}(t)|^{p}\right]

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[|{M}(t)\circ\pi|^{p}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[|{M}(t)|^{p}\right].

The adaptedness condition follows from (ii) in Definition
B.2
.
The proof is complete by observing that in view of property (iv), if
t>s
, then
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. the following chain of equalities hold

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\tilde{M}(t)|\tilde{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[{M}(t)|{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\bigr]\circ\pi=M(s)\circ\pi=\tilde{M}(s).

∎

The main result of this section is the following.

Proposition B.5
.

Assume that
M=(M_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a square integrable purely discontinuous
\mathbb{R}
-valued martingale on a filtered probability space

\mathfrak{A}=\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}},{\mathbb{F}}\bigr)
, where
{\mathbb{F}}=({\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
.
Assume that a filtered probability space

\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}=\bigl(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathscr{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}}\bigr)
, where
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}=(\tilde{\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
,
is an extension of the former. Define a process
\tilde{M}=(\tilde{M}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by (
B.2
),
where
\pi\colon\tilde{\Omega}\to\Omega
is a map as in Definition
B.2
. Then

\tilde{M}
is a purely discontinuous square integrable martingale on
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Proof.

In view of the characterisation (
6.5
) in Section
6.1
,
\tilde{M}
is a purely discontinuous (square integrable)
\mathbb{R}
-valued martingale if and only if for every
t>0
, almost surely

[\tilde{M}](t)=\sum_{s\leq t}(\Delta\tilde{M}(s))^{2}.

(B.3)

To prove the above, we choose and fix
t>0
and observe that in view of the equality (
6.1
) we have,
in
L^{1}
over the partitions of
[0,t]
with mesh converging to
0
,

\displaystyle[\tilde{M}](t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\tilde{M}(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t)-\tilde{M}(t_{i-1}^{n}\wedge t)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\lim\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left({M}(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t)\circ\pi-{M}(t_{i-1}^{n}\wedge t)\circ\pi\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\lim\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left({M}(t_{i}^{n}\wedge t)-{M}(t_{i-1}^{n}\wedge t)\right)^{2}\circ\pi=[{M}](t)\circ\pi.

Hence, because
{M}
is a purely discontinuous (square integrable) real martingale we infer that

\displaystyle[\tilde{M}](t)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\sum_{s\leq t}(\Delta{M}(s))^{2}\Bigr)\circ\pi

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\sum_{s\leq t}(\Delta{M}(s))^{2}\circ\pi\Bigr)=\sum_{s\leq t}(\Delta{M}(s)\circ\pi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\leq t}(\Delta\tilde{M}(s))^{2}.

This proves (
B.3
) and therefore, the proof is complete.
∎

Appendix C
Point processes

Our presentation of the point processes will follow
[
34
, Section 9 of Chapter I, p. 43]
.

Definition C.1
.

Suppose that
X
is a non-empty set. A point function on
X
is a partial function,
i.e.
, a functional binary relation,

p\subset(0,\infty)\times X.

which is at most a countable set.

If
p
is a point function on
X
then we define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Dom}(p)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{p}:=\bigl\{t\in(0,\infty):p\cap(\{t\}\times X)\not=\emptyset\}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{t\in(0,\infty):\mbox{ there exists an element }x\in X:(t,x)\in p\}.

If
t\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)
, then we will denote by
p(t)
the value of
p
at
t
, i.e. the unique element
x\in X
such that
(t,x)\in p
.

Moreover, when we will write
p(t)
we will implicitly understand that
t\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)
.

The family of all point functions on
X
will be denoted by
\Pi_{X}
.

Remark C.2
.

Often a point function is defined differently, see e.g.
[
54
]
. One introduces an auxiliary set
X^{\ast}:=X\cup\{\partial\}
with
\partial
an element not belonging to
X
. Then a point function on
X
is defined
to be a function
p:(0,\infty)\to X^{\ast}
such that the set
\bigl\{t\in(0,\infty):p(t)\in X\bigr\}
, denoted by
D_{p}
, is at most countable. Obviously, these two definitions are equivalent.

Example C.3
.

Assume that
X
is a Banach space and
f:[0,\infty)\to X
is a càdlàg   function. Then, see e.g.
[
67
, Lemma 3.1.24]
the set

J_{p}:=\bigl\{t\in[0,\infty):\Delta f(t)\not=0\bigr\}

is at most countable. Let us define a function
p\subset(0,\infty)\times X
by putting

\displaystyle\mathrm{Dom}(p)

\displaystyle=J_{p},

\displaystyle p(t)

\displaystyle=\Delta f(t),\;\;\mbox{ if }t\in J_{p}.

Then
p
is a point function (on both
X
and
X\setminus\{0\}
.).

Definition C.4
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a measure space and
p
is a point function on
X
. We put

\eta_{p}((0,t]\times U):=\#\bigl\{s\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)\cap(0,t]:p(s)\in U\bigr\},\;\;t>0,\;\;U\in\mathscr{B}_{X}.

(C.1)

Following
[
34
, page 43]
we have the following result.

Proposition C.5
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and
p
is a point function on
X
.
The function
\eta_{p}
defined above in (
C.1
) has a unique extension to a
\sigma
-additive measure

\mathscr{B}(0,\infty)\otimes\mathscr{B}_{X}\to\bar{\mathbb{N}}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\},

where
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is the Borel
\sigma
-field on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Moreover, this unique extension is of the following form

\sum_{(t,x)\in p}\delta_{(t,x)}.

In other words, this extension is the counting measure on
(0,\infty)\times X
associated with
p
and it will be denoted by
\eta_{p}
.

The following exercises can be found useful by some attentive readers.

Exercise C.6
.

In the framework of Defintion
C.4
, if
U\in\mathscr{B}_{X}
, then the function

(0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\eta_{p}((0,t]\times U)\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}

is weakly-increasing and right-continuous.

Moreover, a number
t_{0}\in(0,\infty)
is a discontinuity point of it if and only if
t_{0}\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)
and
p(t_{0})\in U
.

Exercise C.7
.

Show that formula (
C.1
) is equivalent to

\eta_{p}((0,t]\times U)=\sum_{s>0}\mathds{1}_{(0,t]\times U}\bigl(s,p(s)\bigr),\;\;t>0,\;\;U\in\mathscr{B}_{X},

(C.2)

with an obvious understanding that
\mathds{1}_{(0,t]\times U}\bigl(s,p(s)\bigr)=1
if and only if
\bigl(s,p(s)\bigr)\in(0,t]\times U
if and only if
s\in(0,t]\cap\mathrm{Dom}(p)
and
p(s)\in U
.

Deduce that

\iint\mathds{1}_{(0,t]\times U}\eta_{p}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u)=\sum_{s>0}\mathds{1}_{(0,t]\times U}\bigl(s,p(s)\bigr),\;\;t>0,\;\;U\in\mathscr{B}_{X},

(C.3)

or

\iint_{(0,t]\times U}\eta_{p}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u)=\sum_{s>0}\mathds{1}_{(0,t]\times U}\bigl(s,p(s)\bigr),\;\;t>0,\;\;U\in\mathscr{B}_{X},

(C.4)

It is obvious that if
p
and
q
are two point functions on
X
with
p=q
, then the measures
\eta_{p}
and
\eta_{q}
are equal. We have also a converse assertion.

Proposition C.8
.

Suppose that singletons are
\mathscr{B}_{X}
-measurable. If
p
and
q
are two point functions on
X
such that the measures
\eta_{p}
and
\eta_{q}
are equal, i.e. for every
U\in\mathscr{B}_{X}
and every
t>0
,

\#\bigl\{s\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)\cap(0,t]:p(s)\in U\bigr\}=\#\bigl\{s\in\mathrm{Dom}(q)\cap(0,t]:q(s)\in U\bigr\},

(C.5)

or, equivalently,

\sum_{s\in(0,t]}\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{Dom}(p)\times U}(s,p(s))=\sum_{s\in(0,t]}\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{Dom}(q)\times U}(s,q(s))

(C.6)

then

p=q.

Proof of Proposition
C.8
.

Let us choose and fix
t>0
. Then, since (
C.5
) holds for every
U\in\mathscr{B}_{X}
we infer that

\bigl\{p(s):s\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)\cap(0,t]\bigr\}=\bigl\{q(s):s\in\mathrm{Dom}(q)\cap(0,t]\bigr\}.

Since the above equality holds also for every
r<t
we infer that

\bigl\{p(s):s\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)\cap[t,t]\bigr\}=\bigl\{q(s):s\in\mathrm{Dom}(q)\cap[t,t]\bigr\}.

This obviously implies that

t\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)\iff t\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)\mbox{ and if so }p(t)=q(t).

The proof is complete.
∎

In the framework of Example
C.3
Proposition
C.8
yields the following result.

Corollary C.9
.

Assume that
X
is a Banach space and
f,g:[0,\infty)\times\Omega\to X
are two càdlàg   processes such that for
\mathbb{P}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega

\displaystyle\sum_{s>0}\mathds{1}_{(0,t]\times A}(s,\Delta f(s,\omega))

\displaystyle=\sum_{s>0}\mathds{1}_{(0,t]\times A}(s,\Delta g(s))

(C.7)

\displaystyle\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,\infty),\;\;A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbf{X}\setminus\{0\}).

Then,
for
\mathbb{P}
-almost every
\omega\in\Omega
,

\displaystyle\Delta f(t,\omega)

\displaystyle=\Delta g(t,\omega),\quad\mbox{ for every }t\in[0,\infty).

(C.8)

By
\mathscr{B}(\Pi_{X})
we denote the
\sigma
-field on
\Pi_{X}
generated by the family of maps

\Pi_{X}\ni p\mapsto\eta_{p}((0,t]\times U)\in\bar{\mathbb{N}},\;\;t>0,\;\;U\in\mathscr{B}_{X}.

Equivalently,
\mathscr{B}(\Pi_{X})
is the smallest
\sigma
-field on
\Pi_{X}
such that the maps above are measurable.

Remark C.10
.

Compare with
[
35
, Definition II.1.13 ]
.
Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and
p
is point function on
X
.

Then the function
\eta_{p}
satisfies the following condition

\eta_{p}(\{t\}\times X)\leq 1.

Indeed, by Definition
C.4
and Proposition
C.5
we have

\eta_{p}(\{t\}\times X)=\#\bigl\{s\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)\cap\{t\}:p(s)\in X\bigr\}=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{ if }t\in\mathrm{Dom}(p),\\
0,&\mbox{ if }t\in\mathrm{Dom}(p),\end{cases}.

Definition C.11
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and

\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
is a probability space. A random point process (on
X
and on
\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
) is a map

p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}

which is
{\mathscr{F}}/\mathscr{B}(\Pi_{X})
-measurable.
Hereafter, when there is no ambiguity we just say a point process instead of random point process.

If
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}
is a point process, then
we will write
p(t,\omega)
for the value of
p(\omega)
at
t\in D(p(\omega))
.
Moreover, see Definition
C.1
, when we will write
p(t,\omega)
we will implicitly imply that
t\in\mathrm{Dom}(p(\omega))
.

Remark C.12
.

Using the approach from
[
54
]
, a point process (on
X
and on
\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
) is a map

p:(0,\infty)\times\Omega\to X^{\ast}

such that for every
\omega\in\Omega
the set

\bigl\{t\in(0,\infty):p(t,\omega)\in X\bigr\}=:\mathrm{D}_{p(\omega)}

is at most countable and, for all
t>0
and
U\in\mathscr{B}_{X}
, the map

\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\#\bigl\{s\in(0,t]:p(s,\omega)\in U\bigr\},

is
{\mathscr{F}}/\mathscr{B}_{X}
-measurable.

The following example is a random version of Example
C.3
and is related to
[
35
, Proposition I.1.32]

Example C.13
.

Assume that
X
is a Banach space and
\xi:[0,\infty)\times\Omega\to X
is a càdlàg   process. Since the set, see e.g.
[
67
, Lemma 3.1.24]
,

A(\omega):=\bigl\{t\in[0,\infty):\Delta\xi(t,\omega)\not=0\bigr\},\;\;\omega\in\Omega,

is at most countable, we can define a random function
p:(0,\infty)\times\Omega\to X
by

p(t,\omega)=\begin{cases}\Delta\xi(t,\omega),\;\;&\mbox{ if }t\in A(\omega),\\
\partial,\;\;&\mbox{ if }t\in(0,\infty)\setminus A(\omega).\end{cases}

Then
p
is a point process, in the sense of
[
54
]
, (on both
X
and
X\setminus\{0\}
.).

Let us point out, see
[
35
, Proposition I.1.3]
, that if additionally
\xi
is an
\mathbb{F}
-adapted process, then the random set
A
is thin, i.e., there exists a sequence
(\tau_{n})
of
\mathbb{F}
-stopping times such that
[[\tau_{n}]]\cap[[\tau_{m}]]=\emptyset
for
n\not=m
and

A=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[[\tau_{n}]].

We can prove the following result.

Proposition C.14
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and

\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
is a probability space.
Then a map

p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}

is a point process if and only if for all
t>0
and
U\in\mathscr{B}_{X}
the map

\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\eta_{p(\omega)}((0,t]\times U)\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}

is
\mathscr{F}
-measurable.

We now recall some definitions from
[
39
, Definition 3.4.2]
and
[
35
, Definitions II.1.3 and II.1.13]
.

Definition C.15
.

Suppose that

\mathfrak{A}=\bigl(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P},\mathbb{F}\bigr),\mbox{ with }\mathbb{F}=(\mathscr{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0},

is a filtered probability space
and
(E,\mathscr{E})
is a non-empty measurable space.

•

A
random measure
is a mapping

\eta:\Omega\times\bigl(\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathscr{E}\bigr)\to\mathbb{R}

such that the following two conditions are satisfied.

(i)

for every
\omega\in\Omega
, the function

\eta(\omega,\cdot):\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathscr{E}\to\mathbb{R}

is a measure such that
\eta(\omega;\{0\}\times E)=0
for all
\omega\in\Omega
;

(ii)

for every
B\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathscr{E}
, the function
\eta(\cdot,B):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable.

Suppose from now on that
\eta:\Omega\times\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathscr{E}\to\mathbb{R}
is a random measure and put

\Gamma_{\eta}:=\{A\in\mathscr{E}:\mathbb{E}\,\left\lvert\eta([0,t]\times A)\right\rvert<\infty\mbox{ for all }t\geq 0\}.

(C.9)

The random measure
\eta
is called

•

\sigma
-finite
if and only if there exists a sequence
(U_{n})
of elements of
\Gamma_{\eta}
such that

\mathbb{P}
-almost surely
\eta(U_{n})<\infty
and

U_{n}\nearrow E
;

•

integer-valued
if and only if

(i)

for all
(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,\infty)
,

\eta(\omega;\{t\}\times E)\leq 1;

(ii)

for every
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathscr{E}
the random variable
\eta(\cdot;A)
takes values in
\bar{\mathbb{N}}
;

(iii)

\eta
is optional, i.e., for every
A\in\mathscr{E}
, the process

\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\ni(\omega,t)\mapsto\eta(\omega;[0,t]\times A)\in\mathbb{R},

is

\mathbb{F}
-optional;

(iv)

\eta
is
\sigma
-finite.

•

\mathbb{F}
-
adapted
if and only if for every
A\in\Gamma_{\eta}
the process

\eta_{A}:\Omega\times\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\ni(\omega,t)\mapsto\eta(\omega;[0,t]\times A)

is
\mathbb{F}
-adapted;

•

a
martingale random measure
if and only if for every
A\in\Gamma_{\eta}
, the process
\eta_{A}
is an
\mathbb{F}
-martingale;

Proposition C.16
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space,

\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
is a probability space
and
p
is a point process on
X
and on
\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
.

Then the function defined by

\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\eta_{p(\omega)}((0,t]\times U)\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}

for
t>0
and
U\in\mathscr{B}_{X}
, extends to an "integer-valued random measure" in the sense of the above definition, see also

[
35
, Definition II.1.13]
.

Definition C.17
.

Suppose that
X
is a non-empty set and
p
is a point function on
X
. Given
t\geq 0
we define
\theta_{t}p
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Dom}(\theta_{t}p)

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{s\in(0,\infty):s+t\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)\bigr\}=(\mathrm{Dom}(p)-t)\cap(0,\infty),

(C.10)

\displaystyle\theta_{t}p(s)

\displaystyle:=p(s+t),\;\;s\in\mathrm{Dom}(\theta_{t}p).

In other words, if
\mathrm{Dom}(p)=\{s_{i}\}
, then
\mathrm{Dom}(\theta_{t}p)=\{s_{i}-t:s_{i}>t\}
and
\theta_{t}p(s_{i}-t)=p(s_{i})
.

We have the following two results.

Lemma C.18
.

Suppose that
X
is a non-empty set,
p
is a point function on
X
and
t\geq 0
. Then

\eta_{\theta_{t}p}\bigl((0,s]\times U\bigr)=\eta_{p}\bigl((0,t+s]\times U\bigr)-\eta_{p}\bigl((0,t]\times U\bigr),\;\;s>0,U\in\mathscr{B}_{X}.

Proof of Lemma
C.18
.

We omit the proof because the assertion of the lemma follows from straightforward calculations.

∎

Proposition C.19
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and

\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
is a probability space.
If a map
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}

is a point process, then for every
t\geq 0
, the map

\theta_{p}:\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\theta_{p(\omega)}\in\Pi_{X}

is also a point process.

Proof of Proposition
C.19
.

Follows from Lemma
C.18
.
∎

Proposition C.20
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and

\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
is a probability space.
If a map
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}
is a point process, we put

n_{p}((0,t]\times U)=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\eta_{p}((0,t]\times U)\bigr],\;\;t>0,\;\;U\in\mathscr{B}_{X}.

Then the function
n_{p}
has a unique extension to a
\sigma
-additive
\bar{\mathbb{N}}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
-valued function on the set
\mathscr{B}(0,\infty)\otimes\mathscr{B}_{X}
.

It is called the intensity measure of the point process
p
.

The nomenclature "intensity measure" is extracted from
[
34
, Definition I.9.1]
. A related and similar name "intensity measure" of an extended Poisson measure is used in
[
35
, Definition II.1.20]
. The notation used there is different.

Definition C.21
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and
\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
is a probability space. A point process
(on
X
and on
\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
)
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}
is called a stationary point process
if and only if for every
t>0
, the random variable
\theta_{t}p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}
has the same law (on
\Pi_{X}
) as the random variable
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}
.

Recall that a Poisson random measure and a Poisson random measure with respect to a filtration
\mathbb{F}
were defined in Definitions
2.1
and
2.2
, respectively.

Definition C.22
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and

\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
is a probability space. A point process
(on
X
and on
\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
)
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}
is called a Poisson point process
if and only if its corresponding random measure
\eta_{p}
is a Poisson random measure.

We have the following well-known classical results.

Proposition C.23
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and

\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
is a probability space. A Poisson point process
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}

(on
X
and on
\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
) is stationary if and only if there exists a non-negative measure
n
on
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})

such that

n_{p}((0,t]\times U)=tn(U),\;\;\mbox{ for all }t>0,\;u\in\mathscr{B}_{X}.

(C.11)

Definition C.24
.

In the frameword of Proposition
C.23
,
the non-negative measure
n
on
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
such that condition (
C.11
) is satisfied
is called the
characteristic measure
of the Poisson point process
p
.

Let us observe that obviously if such a measure
n
exists, then it is unique and it makes sense to speak about a stationary point process
p
with
the characteristic measure
n
.

Proposition C.25
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and

\bigl({\Omega},{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbb{P}}\bigr)
is a probability space. If

n
is a non-negative measure on
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
then a point process
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}
is a stationary Poisson point process with the characteristic measure
n
iff
for all
t>s\geq 0
, every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and all pair-wise disjoint sets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{\ell}\in\mathscr{B}_{X}
the following identity holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[e^{-\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\lambda_{j}\eta_{p}((s,t]\times U_{j})}\Big|\tilde{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=e^{(t-s)\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}(e^{-\lambda_{j}t}-1)n(U_{j})}

(C.12)

where
\tilde{\mathscr{F}}_{s}
is the
\sigma
-field generated by the following family of random variables

\eta_{p}((0,r]\times U):\;\;r\in(0,s],\;\;u\in\mathscr{B}_{X}.

The following exposition is based on the Section II.3, p. 59, of the monograph
[
34
]
.

Definition C.26
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and that

\mathfrak{A}=\bigl(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P},\mathbb{F}\bigr),\mbox{ with }\mathbb{F}=(\mathscr{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0},

is a filtered probability space. We say that a point process
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}
(on
X
defined on
\Omega
) is
\mathbb{F}
-adapted if and only if for every
t>0
and every
Y\in\mathscr{B}_{X}
the random variable

\eta_{p}((0,t]\times U):=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Dom}(p)\cap(0,t]}\mathds{1}_{U}(p(s))

(C.13)

is
\mathscr{F}_{t}
-measurable.

Definition C.27
.

A point process
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}
is
\sigma
-finite if and only if the associated random measure is
\sigma
-finite.
If a point process
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}
(on
X
defined on
\Omega
) is
\sigma
-finite and
\mathbb{F}
-adapted, then we put

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{p}:=\Bigl\{U\in\mathscr{B}_{X}:\;\mathbb{E}\eta_{p}((0,t]\times U)<\infty,\;\;t>0\Bigr\}.

(C.14)

Exercise C.28
.

In the framework of Definition
C.27
, if
U\in\mathbf{\Gamma}_{p}
, then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s

\eta_{p}((0,t]\times U)<\infty\mbox{ for all }t>0.

In what follows we use the usual shorthand

\eta(t,U):=\eta((0,t]\times U).

Proposition C.29
.

Suppose that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and that

\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}=\bigl(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathscr{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}}\bigr),\mbox{ with }\tilde{\mathbb{F}}=(\tilde{\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0},

is a filtered probability space and
p:\Omega\to\Pi_{X}
is a
\sigma
-finite and
\mathbb{F}
-adapted point process (on
X
defined on
\Omega
). Then the following holds.

If
U\in\Gamma_{p}
, then

(0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\eta_{p}((0,t]\times U)

is an
\mathbb{F}
-adapted, integrable and increasing process.

If
U\in\Gamma_{p}
, then there exists a natural
\mathbb{F}
-adapted integrable and increasing process
\widehat{\eta}_{p}(t,U)
,
t\in(0,\infty)
, such that the process

\tilde{\eta}_{p}(t,U)
,
t\in(0,\infty)
, defined by

\tilde{\eta}_{p}(t,U):=\eta_{p}(t,U)-\widehat{\eta}_{p}(t,U),\;\;t>0,

(C.15)

is an
\mathbb{F}
-martingale.

Proof of Proposition
C.29
.

Part (i) is obvious. Part (ii) follows from
[
35
, Theorem II.1.8]
.
∎

Remark C.30
.

In the framework of Proposition
C.29
,

the process

t\mapsto\widehat{\eta}_{p}(t,U)

is not continuous in general, i.e. there exists such examples;

the function
\Gamma_{p}\ni U\mapsto\widehat{\eta}_{p}(t,U)
is in general not
\sigma
-additive, i.e. there exists such examples.
However, it is known, see

[
34
, Theorem I.3.1]
,
that if
X
satsisfies some additional assumptions, e.g.
X
is standard measurable space, see
[
34
, Definition I.3.3]
, e.g. a Borel subset of a Polish space, then there exists a modification of
\widehat{\eta}_{p}
such that for all
t>0
and for almost all
\omega\in\Omega
, the map

\Gamma_{p}\ni U\mapsto\widehat{\eta}_{p}(t,U)\mbox{ is $\sigma$-additive}.

The following definition is copied, word by word, from
[
34
, Definition II.3.1]
.

Definition C.31
.

Let us assume that that
(X,\mathscr{B}_{X})
is a non-empty measure space and that

\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}=\bigl(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathscr{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}}\bigr),\mbox{ with }\tilde{\mathbb{F}}=(\tilde{\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0},

is a filtered probability space.
An
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
-adapted and
\sigma
-finite point process

p:\tilde{\Omega}\to\Pi_{X},

is of
(QL)
class if and only if there exists a family

\widehat{\eta}_{p}=\Bigl\{\widehat{\eta}_{p}(t,U):\,t>0,\;U\in\Gamma_{p}\Bigr\}

such that the following conditions are satisfied.

For every
U\in\Gamma_{p}
,

(0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\widehat{\eta}_{p}(t,U)

is a continuous
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
-adapted and increasing process .

For every
t>0
,
\tilde{\omega}\in\tilde{\Omega}
,

\mathscr{B}_{X}\ni U\mapsto\widehat{\eta}_{p}(t,U;\tilde{\omega})

is,
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s., a
\sigma
